Why a Website Is Not Indexed: A Practical Diagnostic Workflow

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

When a page is missing from search results, the fastest next step is not to change every SEO setting on the site. Pick one important URL, establish what you expect to happen, and follow the evidence from discovery through crawling, indexing, and canonical selection.

This guide uses Google Search Console and Yandex Webmaster as examples of sources of diagnostic information. Interfaces and report labels can change. Check the current guidance for each service and avoid assuming that the two search engines make identical decisions.

Start with a specific page and a clear expectation

Choose an important public URL: a service page, a category, or an article intended for search visitors. Copy the complete address, including the protocol, host, path, trailing slash, and any parameters. Confirm that it is published and that the page is intended to be public.

A page opening in your browser proves that your browser received a response. It does not prove that a search engine received the same content, added that URL to its index, or will display it for every query. Similarly, a search using the site: operator is a useful clue, not a complete inventory of indexed pages.

Inspect the exact URL in the available webmaster tools. Record the date, reported status, any stated reason, and the selected canonical URL if the service provides it. Compare the information separately for each search engine. A live availability check and a report about a previously processed version answer different questions: a successful live test is not proof that the latest version is already indexed.

If an entire site appears affected, sample different page types. When the home page, a category, a product, and a new article all fail in the same way, look for a common infrastructure or configuration change. If only one group is affected, inspect the relevant template or section.

Check the response and redirect chain

Open the page without logging in when it is supposed to be public. With a developer, inspect the HTTP response and the final address after redirects. A browser can display a friendly error template, a login page, or a placeholder even when the underlying response is not what you expect.

Use the symptom to choose the next investigation:

  • A server error or timeout: investigate availability, resource pressure, and logs.
  • A forbidden response or login requirement: confirm whether the restriction is intended and review the relevant access rules.
  • A missing or removed page: determine whether it should be restored or has a relevant replacement.
  • A redirect: check where it ends and whether the destination matches the intended page.
  • A successful response: inspect the actual content and indexing signals; status alone does not guarantee indexing.

For a hypothetical example, a URL ending in /service/ might redirect to /services/. Decide which page is supposed to be primary and inspect the final destination. Redirecting every missing page to the homepage just to avoid an error can send visitors to an unrelated destination.

Security filters may return different responses to different requests. Work with the administrator to examine evidence before changing a rule. Do not open private or staging sections simply to make a crawler pass, and do not trust a user-agent label alone as proof of a search crawler's identity.

Separate crawl restrictions from indexing instructions

The robots.txt file controls crawling for applicable user agents and paths. Check the correct host and protocol, and identify the rule affecting the target page. An old staging rule or a deployment mistake may prevent a search crawler from fetching an intended public section.

A crawl restriction is not the same as reliable access control or guaranteed removal from an index. An address may be known from other sources even if its contents cannot be fetched. Protect confidential content with appropriate authentication and authorization.

Next inspect indexing directives in the HTML and HTTP response headers. A noindex instruction may come from a template, a page-level setting, or server configuration. Find the origin of an unintended directive before changing it, and protect sections that the team deliberately keeps private.

An important interaction: if crawling is blocked, the crawler may not retrieve the page and see a noindex instruction on it. Choose a remedy based on the actual objective: make a public page eligible for indexing or restrict access to private content. Do not apply a blanket fix to both.

Investigate duplicates and canonical signals

Several URLs can represent similar content: versions with parameters, different hosts, or alternate paths. Decide which URL should be primary, then compare the redirect destination, internal links, canonical link, and any canonical information in the webmaster tool.

A canonical link expresses a preference; it does not guarantee that a search engine will select that version. Conflicting signals deserve investigation. A product page pointing to an entire category, or two distinct services pointing to the same canonical address, may reflect a template mistake. It might also be intentional, so establish the business purpose before editing.

Do not assume that every parameterized page should be excluded. Some filters or variations may serve a distinct purpose. Classify them into desired landing pages, duplicates, and operational URLs, then test changes on representative examples.

Look at discovery, sitemaps, and content

Check whether important pages are reachable through useful internal links. An isolated URL may be harder to discover. A sitemap can help communicate desired URLs, but it does not guarantee a crawl, indexing, or ranking.

Keep sitemap entries aligned with the intended public URLs. Review whether listed addresses resolve successfully, redirect unexpectedly, or include deliberately closed pages. A successful sitemap fetch is a property of the file; it is not confirmation that every listed page is indexed.

Inspect the main content. Does it answer a distinct user need? Is it available without a login when the page is intended for public search? If essential text or links depend on JavaScript, use the available inspection tools and developer diagnostics to see what a search engine can actually process in that implementation. One successful browser view does not establish identical behavior for every crawler.

Content quality is not an instant technical switch. Still, removing empty placeholders and resolving near-identical pages helps clarify the purpose of each URL for both visitors and search systems.

Work through a concrete example

Suppose a service page is public but an inspection report says the specific URL is not indexed. The requested address redirects to a second URL, while the canonical link on that page points to a third. The navigation and sitemap still use the first.

  1. Confirm the intended primary URL with the site owner.
  2. Check the final response, public access, relevant crawl rules, and indexing directives.
  3. Align redirects, canonical signals, internal links, and sitemap entries where appropriate.
  4. Test the actual published page and record exactly what changed.
  5. Recheck the same URL in each search system and use a supported recrawl or reprocessing request where appropriate.

This is an illustrative sequence, not proof that conflicting signals are always the cause. If the diagnostic tool reports a different problem, investigate that evidence first.

Keep an issue log after the fix

For each URL, record the page type, intended role, observation date, service-specific status, response and final URL, important directives, change made, and owner. Note a date for a follow-up check.

Do not promise a universal indexing deadline. Search systems may need time to discover and process changes, and submitting the same request repeatedly does not guarantee faster inclusion. If the result has not changed, verify that the deployed page is the intended version and that the original cause was actually removed.

Avoid three common mistakes: removing every restriction without understanding why it exists, treating a sitemap as an indexing command, and changing a whole site after examining one example. A small, documented sample makes it easier to identify a shared cause without exposing private sections or creating new redirect problems.

How to Tell What Claude Code and Codex Forget When They Compact a Session

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Engineer's view. This is a process restart with a handoff note. Whatever the process wrote down or saved to disk comes back. Whatever sat only in memory is gone, and nothing errors. A coding agent's memory is its conversation, so values it read but never wrote down vanish, and it must read them again.

TL;DR. A compacted agent keeps your instructions and its own edits, but loses most of what it only read. Whether a fact survives depends on how it entered the session, so you can predict the loss and tell the summary what to keep.

Explain Like I'm 5

You are building a huge model castle, and your table is full. So you ask a friend to write a
short note about the castle, then you clear the table.

The note says what you were told to build and which towers you finished. It does not list every
piece you looked at in the box. Those are still in the box, so you can look again.

But some pieces you borrowed for a minute, and they went back to a friend's house. If the note
does not mention them, you will not remember them, and you cannot go look.

So before you clear the table, tell your friend what to put in the note.

The Problem

You have shipped this bug already, in a system with no model in it.

Your service held a session cache in process memory. A deploy restarted it. The health check went
green and the error rate stayed flat. Then users started logging in again, because the sessions
never reached disk. Nothing crashed. The process simply came back without the state that had only
lived in memory.

A coding agent has the same split, and it restarts in the middle of your work.

Its working memory is the conversation. When that fills, the agent runs compaction: it
replaces the conversation with a summary and carries on. In my test session, 78,642 tokens became
2,632. That is 97% gone in about twenty seconds, and the session looks healthy afterward.

The agent then keeps working from the note. If a detail is missing, you see no error. You only see
it read the same file twice, or ask something you already answered. Worse, the detail may have
come from a test run or a web page that no longer exists.

You could treat this as bad luck. It is not. What survives follows a rule.

The fix: know which lane each fact is in, tell the summary what to keep, and write anything you
cannot regenerate to a file before compaction runs.

The Fix: Sort Facts by How They Entered the Session

Compaction is a rebuild, not a shortening. The new context comes from three lanes.

What reloads from disk?

Claude Code reloads the root CLAUDE.md, auto memory and the plan file. It also re-reads up to
five recent files, each under 5,000 tokens. Rules with paths: and nested CLAUDE.md files are
different: they entered as messages, so they get summarized. They load again only when the agent
touches a matching file.

What does a budget keep?

Codex's open-source compaction path keeps your recent messages word for word, up to 20,000 tokens,
walking newest to oldest. It adds a summary and re-injects its starting context. Older messages
depend on the summary.

Which facts does the summary drop?

I tested this rather than assume it. I planted facts in real Claude Code sessions, compacted them,
then asked from memory with tools off. Each of three sessions read 25 small config files.

Every instruction survived, and so did all ten edited files, even after a second compaction. The
agent said so plainly when a value was gone, even when told never to answer "unknown".

The summary kept what mattered to the work so far. In an earlier run, the last task counted timeouts
over 5000. Of six values I probed, only the two timeouts over 5000 survived.

What This Means for You

When this matters. You run long sessions in Claude Code, Codex or any agent that summarizes its
history. It matters most when the agent reads something it cannot read again: a failed test run, a
production log, a web page, or the output of a command that changes state.

How it affects you. A file on disk costs you one more read. The agent gets it back and you pay
tokens. A one-time output costs more, because the evidence is gone. The agent will then rerun the
command, or reason from a vague line in the note. Long sessions may lose more than my small test
did. Factory ran probe questions after compression on 36,611 production messages. All three methods
scored worst on tracking files, at 2.19 to 2.45 out of 5, even though each kept instructions well.

What to do about it. Start with no setup. Before you compact, name what matters:

/compact Keep the exact failing test names, error strings and ids.

A line like that, naming the values I needed, took my test from 0 to 36 of 36.

Next, watch for the moment that hurts. When the agent reads something you cannot regenerate, ask it
to save the facts to a notes file. A file sits in the disk lane.

Then fix the repeated cases. Move a rule that must always hold into your root rules file. Add a hook
that restores your working state after every compaction. Finally, audit a real session to see your
own numbers.

Implementing It

The change.

Four jobs, and one person may do all of them.

Job 1, whoever drives the session. Save anything you cannot regenerate, then compact at a task
boundary with a focus. Codex takes a compaction prompt in its config, so the same habit can be set
once there. In ~/.codex/config.toml:

compact_prompt = "Summarize this session. Keep exact error strings, ids, file paths and test names."

Job 2, whoever owns the repo's agent setup. Check which lane your rules are in. A rule with
paths: enters as a message, so its survival is up to the summary until the agent touches a
matching file again. My short test kept one, but the docs warn it can go. If it must hold across
compaction, move it to the root file. Then add a section the summarizer reads, in CLAUDE.md:

# Compact Instructions Keep exact error strings, failing test names, ids and every file edited.

Add a hook for state that changes. A SessionStart hook with the compact matcher runs after
every compaction, and its output joins the new context. In .claude/settings.json:

{ "hooks": { "SessionStart": [ { "matcher": "compact", "hooks": [ { "type": "command", "command": "git status --short; cat notes/session.md 2>/dev/null" } ] } ] } }

Job 3, whoever builds their own agent. Do not trust a summary with the file list. Keep it as data
in your loop, and put it back after you summarize.

def rebuild(history, pinned, ledger, summarize, keep_tokens=20_000): recent = newest_user_messages(history, keep_tokens) note = summarize(history, focus="exact errors, ids, decisions") edited = "Files edited: " + ", ".join(sorted(ledger.edited)) return [*pinned, *recent, edited, note]

Job 4, whoever wants the numbers. code_example.py needs no model. It reads a session transcript,
finds each compaction, and checks which identifiers reached the summary, grouped by lane. Run it on
one of your own sessions:

python3 code_example.py ~/.claude/projects/<project>/<session>.jsonl

An identifier here means an exact string such as a port, path or error code. On 40 compactions
from my own sessions, 4% of the identifiers the agent only read reached the summary, against 19% of those the agent or I wrote. An exact-string match undercounts paraphrase, so
trust the gap more than the percentages.

How you know it worked.

Probe it. Fork a session right after a compaction, turn tools off, and ask for five facts you need.
Offer "UNKNOWN" as an answer so a miss is visible. In Claude Code:

claude -p --resume "$SESSION" --fork-session --tools "" \ "From memory, no tools. Which files did you edit? What was the exact error? Reply UNKNOWN if unsure."

Before your change, count the misses. After it, the same probe should return your focus items
exactly. Also watch re-reads: the same file read twice right after a compaction means the note
missed it.

When Managing Compaction Is the Wrong Tool

When the task is short. A session that never fills its context never compacts. Spending effort on a
hook then buys nothing.

When the facts are on disk and cheap to read. Losing a value from a config file costs one more
read. Asking the summary to keep every value makes the note longer, and a long note costs tokens on
every later turn. Keep what is expensive to get back.

When you should start over. If the next task is unrelated, /clear is better than any summary. Old
context crowds the new work, however well it was compressed.

When the work belongs elsewhere. Send large research to a subagent, so the reads never enter your
main conversation at all.

My test has limits too. It used small synthetic sessions on one model, and I did not run the Codex
side. Run the probe on your own work before you trust these numbers.

Three questions before you add a hook:

  • Which fact, if lost, could the agent not get back?
  • Does that fact reach the model through a file, or only through a message?
  • Would a probe after compaction return it exactly?

Glossary

  • compaction — replacing a conversation with a shorter summary so the agent can keep working.
  • lane — the route a fact takes through compaction: reloaded, kept by budget, or summarized.
  • probe — a question asked after compaction to test whether a fact survived.
  • focus — an instruction telling the summary what to keep, as in /compact with text after it.
  • identifier — an exact string such as a port, id, file path or error code.

Further reading

  • Evaluating Context Compression for AI Agents — Factory Research
  • Explore the context window — Claude Code docs
  • compact.rs in the Codex source — OpenAI Codex repository
  • Re-inject context after compaction — Claude Code docs

Originally published at theaicommit.com, a daily AI engineering lab for software engineers. The original has an interactive version you can play with, and the full runnable code is on GitHub.

EU KIDS Act Proposal Sets New Age and Safety Rules for Online Platforms

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The European Commission has proposed the EU KIDS Act, a Regulation that would establish EU-wide age rules and safety-by-design obligations for online platforms and related services used by children and teenagers. Published on September 17, 2026, the proposal would affect how social platforms, apps, online games, app stores and AI companions verify age, configure feeds and interactions, and handle data relating to minors.

The central shift is clear: platforms would need to demonstrate that services are age-appropriate and safe by design, rather than placing most responsibility on children, parents or regulators after harm occurs. The Commission's official EU KIDS Act announcement presents the proposal as part of a broader effort to harmonize child-safety rules across the EU and avoid differing national requirements.

For companies building services that may attract minors, the proposal is not simply an age-gate issue. It would place product design, moderation, data practices and parental controls closer to the center of compliance planning. The Act remains a proposal, and the Commission's public consultation is open until December 3, 2026.

What the EU KIDS Act would change

The proposal sets out age-based rules for social media accounts. Children under 13 would be prohibited from holding accounts. Users aged 13 and 14 could use guardian-assisted accounts, subject to strict controls. From age 15, users could have autonomous accounts only on services considered safe by design.

Age group Proposed social media account rule Key condition Under 13 Accounts prohibited No social media account access 13 to 14 Guardian-assisted accounts Daily time limit and parental approval of contacts 15 and over Autonomous accounts permitted Service must be safe by design

The Act would also reverse the practical burden of proof for platforms. Instead of relying chiefly on users or authorities to identify unsafe experiences, providers would have to show that their service is appropriate for minors. Very Large Online Platforms, defined in the proposal as platforms with 45 million or more monthly users, would face independent audits.

Age assurance is a core implementation issue. The Commission proposes certified age checks that use privacy-preserving approaches, including zero-knowledge techniques. The package also envisages a free EU age-verification option and eventual use of the European Digital Identity Wallet. These mechanisms matter because platforms would need to establish age and, where relevant, guardianship without unnecessarily collecting or retaining sensitive personal information.

Safety-by-design becomes a product requirement

The EU KIDS Act proposal reaches beyond account creation. It would require platforms, apps and AI companions to build safer defaults into feeds, interactions and feature design. The proposal specifically targets addictive design patterns, including endless autoplay, intrusive notifications, streaks and rewards tied to engagement.

For teams responsible for digital products, the most consequential changes would likely include:

  • Feed and notification redesign to avoid prohibited engagement mechanisms.
  • Safer default settings rather than expecting younger users to locate and enable protections.
  • Limited profiling of minors and clearer user controls over relevant settings.
  • Contact and interaction safeguards, including parental approval of contacts for guardian-assisted accounts.
  • Age-rating and verification integration in online games and app stores.
  • Pre-launch risk testing for AI companions, alongside restrictions on default activation and coercive interactions involving minors.

The proposal's treatment of AI chatbots and AI companions is particularly notable. It recognizes that conversational systems can create risks distinct from traditional social feeds. Services used by minors would need safeguards designed to prevent coercive interactions, and providers would need to test risks before launch. The proposal would integrate with existing EU frameworks, including the Digital Services Act and the AI Act, rather than operate as an isolated child-safety regime.

What compliance could mean for smaller platform teams

The research does not provide cost estimates, so businesses should be wary of treating any single budget figure as definitive. However, the proposal points to several likely cost and operational drivers: integrating certified age checks, adapting account flows, revising recommendation and notification systems, building parental controls, updating data practices, and testing products for risks to minors.

Those changes may be especially material for a smaller company that has relied on standard analytics, third-party identity tools or engagement-focused product patterns. A business operating an app, game, community or AI experience should first determine whether minors can access it in the EU, whether the service is designed to attract them, and which product features create contact, profiling or engagement risks.

A practical early review can focus on four questions:

  1. Who can access the service? Map current age rules, onboarding flows and the ability to distinguish a guardian-assisted account from an autonomous one.
  2. Which features shape behaviour? Review autoplay, notifications, streaks, rewards, recommendations, direct messaging and contact discovery.
  3. What data is used for younger audiences? Identify profiling, analytics and data flows connected to minors, then assess whether they can be limited or redesigned.
  4. How would safeguards be tested? Document risk testing for AI companions and other features that can influence or contact younger users.

The proposal's use of the DSA enforcement framework adds weight to these preparations. The Commission would directly supervise the largest platforms, and non-compliance could lead to fines of up to 6% of worldwide turnover. Smaller services may not face the same direct supervision model, but the proposed safety-by-design duties would still make early product assessment valuable.

For businesses considering AI features, age assurance and safe interaction design should not be treated as a late legal review. They influence the choice of tools, the information an AI system can access, default user settings and the testing required before a feature reaches younger users.

If your product uses AI, automation or personalised experiences, Scalevise can help translate emerging requirements into practical product and workflow decisions. Our AI implementation consultancy for safer digital services can identify high-risk user journeys, assess feasible safeguards and prioritize changes before they become expensive rework. Request an AI implementation consultation.

Frequently Asked Questions

What is the EU KIDS Act?

The EU KIDS Act is a European Commission proposal for a Regulation intended to strengthen online safety for minors through EU-wide age rules, age verification and safety-by-design obligations for platforms and related services.

Would the EU KIDS Act ban social media for children under 13?

The proposal would prohibit social media accounts for children under 13. It would allow guardian-assisted accounts for users aged 13 and 14, while autonomous accounts from age 15 would be limited to services considered safe by design.

What product features would the proposal restrict?

The proposal would prohibit addictive designs such as endless autoplay, intrusive notifications, streaks and rewards for engagement. It would also require safer defaults, limited profiling and explicit user controls.

How would the proposal affect AI companions used by minors?

AI chatbots and AI companions used by minors would face restrictions on default activation, safeguards against coercive interactions and pre-launch risk testing under the proposal.

When could the EU KIDS Act take effect?

The European Commission has published a formal proposal, not a final Regulation. Public consultation on the proposal is open until December 3, 2026, and the supplied material does not provide a final adoption or application date.

Conclusion

The EU KIDS Act proposal would make child safety a more direct product-design obligation for online services operating in the EU. Its proposed age rules, privacy-preserving verification and restrictions on engagement features signal that platforms should begin examining how minors access and experience their products while the legislative process continues.

Terraform Testing in 2026: Unit Tests With terraform test and Mock Providers

TL;DR: Most Terraform changes are still reviewed by reading a plan and hoping. The native test framework (terraform test, stable since Terraform 1.6) changes that: test files declare inputs, run a plan or apply, and assert on the results — and with mock providers (since 1.7) the logic tests run in CI in seconds with no cloud account and no credentials. Test your modules' contracts — outputs, conditional resources, naming and tagging rules — not the cloud provider's behaviour. Keep a small number of real apply tests for critical modules in an ephemeral sandbox, and let linters and scanners do their separate jobs alongside.

Originally published on DevOps Enginer.

The problem: the plan is not a test

Application code gets unit tests as a matter of course. Infrastructure code — which can delete a database just as dead as any application bug — usually gets a human skimming terraform plan output in a pull request. That review catches typos. It does not catch the conditional that inverted last refactor, the variable default that quietly changed a bucket from private to public, or the count logic that creates zero replicas of the thing production needs three of.

The reason was tooling: for years, testing Terraform meant heavyweight frameworks or applying to a real account and inspecting by hand. The native framework removes the excuse. Tests live next to the module, run with one command, and — the decisive part — can run against mocked providers, so the suite executes like unit tests: fast, hermetic, credential-free.

What is worth testing (and what is not)

Test your module's decisions, not the provider's implementation:

  • Outputs and contracts. Callers depend on output values and shapes. If bucket_name changes format, downstream code breaks — a test should break first.
  • Conditional logic. Every count, for_each, and ternary is a decision your module makes: versioning on/off, optional resources created or skipped, single vs multi-AZ shapes. Each branch deserves a run.
  • Naming, tagging, and policy invariants. The rules platform teams actually care about: names compose from project/environment correctly, required tags are always present, encryption is enabled, nothing is public unless an explicit variable says so.
  • Variable edge cases. Empty lists, zero counts, nulls — the inputs real callers will eventually pass.

Do not test that AWS creates an S3 bucket when asked, that an attribute AWS generates looks a certain way beyond your control, or other provider internals. Those tests are slow, brittle, and verify someone else's software.

The framework in one module

A small module to test:

# main.tf variable "project" { type = string } variable "environment" { type = string } variable "enable_versioning" { type = bool default = true } resource "aws_s3_bucket" "this" { bucket = "${var.project}-${var.environment}-assets" } resource "aws_s3_bucket_versioning" "this" { bucket = aws_s3_bucket.this.id versioning_configuration { status = var.enable_versioning ? "Enabled" : "Suspended" } } output "bucket_name" { value = aws_s3_bucket.this.bucket }

And its test file (conventionally in a tests/ directory inside the module):

# tests/bucket.tftest.hcl mock_provider "aws" {} variables { project = "shop" environment = "prod" } run "versioning_on_by_default" { command = plan assert { condition = aws_s3_bucket_versioning.this.versioning_configuration[0].status == "Enabled" error_message = "Versioning must be enabled by default" } assert { condition = output.bucket_name == "shop-prod-assets" error_message = "Bucket name must compose project and environment" } } run "versioning_can_be_disabled" { command = plan variables { enable_versioning = false } assert { condition = aws_s3_bucket_versioning.this.versioning_configuration[0].status == "Suspended" error_message = "Versioning should suspend when explicitly disabled" } }

Run it with terraform test from the module root. The anatomy:

  • mock_provider "aws" {} replaces the real provider. Terraform generates fake computed values, so a plan (or even an apply) runs without credentials, network, or a cloud account — in milliseconds.
  • variables {} at file level sets shared inputs; a run block can override them per case.
  • run blocks are the test cases. command = plan is the default workhorse; command = apply executes against the (possibly mocked) provider for shape checks that only appear after apply.
  • assert pairs a condition over any value in the configuration — resources, outputs, variables, locals — with the error_message a human reads when it fails. Write the messages as explanations, not echoes.

Where a mocked computed value needs to be specific — an ARN a policy references, an ID another resource consumes — override_resource and override_data blocks pin those values inside a run. Use them sparingly; a test that overrides everything asserts nothing.

Mock providers: what they change, and their honest limits

Before mocks, a "unit test" of a module still configured the real provider, which meant credentials in CI, provider API calls in every plan, and suites measured in minutes. Mocks invert all three: the pipeline job needs no cloud credentials at all for the test stage (which, per the keyless-CI pattern in the GitHub Actions OIDC guide on this site, is exactly where you want the fewest credentials), tests run at unit-test speed, and nobody's sandbox account accumulates test debris.

The limits are equally clear, and stating them keeps the suite trustworthy:

  • Mocks validate your configuration logic against the provider's schema, not against the real API. A value the real AWS API would reject (an invalid name pattern, an unsupported combination) can sail through a mocked plan.
  • Data sources return generated values unless overridden — tests depending on a data source's real content need overrides or a real read.
  • Provider-side quirks and eventual-consistency behaviour are, by definition, absent.

So the strategy is layered: mocked tests for logic, in bulk; a few real tests for reality, on purpose.

The top of the pyramid: real apply tests, kept few

For a critical module — the network foundation, the shared database module — a mocked plan cannot prove the one thing that matters: it actually builds. Keep a small suite of command = apply runs against the real provider in a sandbox account with three disciplines:

  • Ephemeral by design. Tests create, assert, and the framework destroys what it created at the end of the run (that is the framework's contract for test-created resources). Verify the destroy happened; orphaned test resources are a cost and security smell — the state-management guide's hygiene rules apply to test state too.
  • Separate credentials, narrow scope. The sandbox role can create and destroy only the resource types under test, in the sandbox account only. The OIDC guide's per-environment role pattern fits exactly here.
  • Scheduled, not per-commit. Real applies take minutes and cost real (if small) money. Run them nightly or on module release, while the mocked suite gates every pull request.

Where tests sit among the other tools

Terraform already has a quality neighbourhood, and the test framework does not evict anyone:

  • Variable validation blocks — the module rejecting bad input at plan time. First line of defence, and the test suite should cover that they fire.
  • terraform fmt / validate — shape and syntax. Free; run always.
  • tflint and friends — lint rules, naming conventions, deprecated syntax. Style and hygiene, not behaviour.
  • Checkov / tfsec-style scanners — policy and security posture of the configuration. They check what the config says; tests check what the module decides given inputs. Complementary, not overlapping.
  • terraform test — behaviour: outputs, conditionals, invariants across input variations.

A sane pipeline order: fmt check → validate → lint/scan → terraform test (mocked) → plan for human review. Each stage is cheap; together they mean the human reviews intent, not correctness arithmetic.

Modules are where tests pay rent

For a root configuration used once, tests are nice. For modules — shared, versioned, consumed by teams who did not write them — they are the contract enforcement mechanism. The modules guide in this series covers structuring reusable modules; tests are what let a maintainer refactor a module's internals while proving its callers see the same outputs, the same resource decisions, the same invariants. That confidence is precisely what a module library runs on, and it is why test files should ship inside the module repository, versioned with the code they describe.

Common mistakes

  • Testing the provider. Asserting that a mocked (or real) AWS resource has AWS-chosen properties teaches the suite to fail for other people's changes. Assert your decisions.
  • Overriding everything. Heavy override_resource use pins the fake world so completely that the test passes regardless of the configuration. Overrides are scalpels for specific computed values, not a substitute world.
  • One giant run block. A failed assert should name the broken decision. Many small runs with descriptive names beat one mega-run with forty asserts.
  • Apply tests against shared accounts. Test resources in an account other teams use will eventually collide, linger, or be "cleaned up" by someone mid-run. Sandbox account, always.
  • Mocked-only confidence for critical paths. The suite is green, the module cannot actually apply. Keep the few real apply tests for the modules whose failure would hurt.
  • Ignoring test state. terraform test manages its own state for runs, but interrupted real applies can leave residue; a periodic sandbox sweep belongs in the routine.
  • Writing tests once, at the end. The suite earns its keep when a refactor lands six months later. Add the run block when you add the conditional — that is when the intent is clearest and cheapest to encode.

FAQ

terraform test or Terratest?
They answer different questions. terraform test is native, HCL-based, and ideal for module logic and contracts — no Go toolchain, no extra framework. Terratest (Go) remains strong for end-to-end verification that spans tools and real cloud behaviour beyond one module. Many teams use both: native tests in every module repo, Terratest-style end-to-end checks for platform assemblies.

Does terraform test cost money?
Mocked and plan-only runs create nothing and cost nothing. command = apply runs against a real provider create real resources for the duration of the test — priced accordingly, then destroyed by the framework. Keep them few, small, and in a sandbox.

Can tests run in CI without cloud credentials?
Mocked-provider tests: yes, completely — that is their headline feature. Plan runs against a real provider still need whatever credentials that provider requires to configure itself, which is one more reason the mocked suite should carry the bulk of the assertions.

Where do test files live?
In the module root or a tests/ subdirectory (the conventional choice), as *.tftest.hcl. terraform test discovers them in both places; keep one convention per repository.

What about OpenTofu?
OpenTofu implements the same test framework model — test files, run blocks, and mock providers are compatible concepts, so the approach in this guide carries across. Check your version's documentation for exact feature parity before assuming a specific block behaves identically.

NIST Just Rewrote the Post-Quantum Deadline. Your RSA-3072 Plan Is Out of Date

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On October 27, NIST published the final version of IR 8547, Transition to Post-Quantum Cryptography Standards. If you followed the draft from November 2024, you might think you know what it says. You probably don't, because the final version changed the one thing that mattered most.

The draft deprecated only the 112-bit parameter sets after 2030. That left a comfortable reading: RSA-3072, P-256 ECDSA, and Ed25519 looked acceptable until 2035. The final report closes that door. Every approved quantum-vulnerable public-key algorithm is deprecated after 2030 and disallowed under NIST standards and guidelines after 2035, regardless of key size.

That is not a small edit. It moves the algorithms most products actually ship today into the deprecation category, with just over four years on the clock.

What the schedule actually says

For signatures, RSA, ECDSA, and EdDSA are all deprecated after 2030 and disallowed after 2035. For key establishment, finite-field Diffie-Hellman, elliptic-curve Diffie-Hellman, MQV, and RSA key transport get the same treatment. The dates are the same as the draft. The scope is much wider.

"Deprecated" in NIST usage does not mean banned. It means the algorithm may still be used, but there is a security risk, and the data owner has to examine that risk and decide whether to continue. In practice, that turns into a documented risk-acceptance obligation. For federal high-value assets and high-impact systems, the grace period is shorter than it looks: an executive order from June 2026 requires post-quantum key establishment by December 31, 2030, and post-quantum signatures by December 31, 2031. Those systems do not get until 2035. They get 2030 and 2031.

And the report does not stop at federal systems. NIST explicitly positions it as a blueprint for critical infrastructure and other organizations. The deadlines reach private vendors through procurement, not through NIST directly.

How a government report becomes your vendor requirement

NIST IR 8547 is not binding on private companies. It reaches them through five mechanisms, and together they explain why this report functions as a vendor requirement in practice.

First, the executive order directs the FAR Council to propose a rule requiring covered contractors to comply with NIST's post-quantum FIPS by December 31, 2030. That proposed rule is due around December 19, 2026, under a 180-day clock. Second, OMB Memorandum M-26-15, issued in June 2026, tells agencies to build post-quantum integration into product requirements and to settle post-quantum responsibilities with FedRAMP cloud providers. Agencies owed their migration plans by October 22, 2026. Third, CISA published a list of product categories back in January 2026, from web browsers to endpoint encryption, where agencies "should acquire only PQC-capable products." Fourth, NIST is revising its module validation processes to move faster. Fifth, CISA owes minimum elements for a cryptographic bill of materials by around March 19, 2027.

If you sell software, cloud services, or hardware into any of those categories, expect to be evaluated on post-quantum capability in federal acquisitions now, not after the FAR rule lands. And vendors are unlikely to keep separate federal and commercial cryptographic baselines for long. One baseline wins, and it will be the post-quantum one.

A Government Accountability Office report from October found that none of the 24 agencies it reviewed had fully addressed all three preparatory practices: building a cryptographic inventory, identifying funding, and planning testing. The people with the biggest budgets and the clearest mandate are behind. That should tell smaller teams something: this is not a task you start in 2029.

The hybrid question finally has an answer

For builders, the most useful clarification in the final report is about hybrid key establishment, the approach that pairs ML-KEM with a classical exchange like X25519. Until now, it was unclear how such constructions would fare in FIPS 140 validation once the classical half became deprecated or disallowed. The final report answers it: as long as at least one input to an approved composite key-establishment technique comes from an approved scheme, the combination can be validated. A hybrid mode does not lose its validation status because its classical half ages out.

That removes the last respectable excuse to wait. You can ship hybrid now without painting yourself into a validation corner later.

Two cautions, though. A validated module that supports hybrid ML-KEM does not prove that a given deployment actually negotiates it. Capability and enablement are different things, and buyers should check which one they are getting. And on hybrid signatures, NIST is more careful: it will accommodate dual signatures in validation but notes that their security properties are complex and need careful analysis. Signatures are the slower, more operationally disruptive half of this transition. They touch certificate authorities, firmware signing, hardware security modules, and smart cards, and none of those move quickly.

NIST also named ML-KEM-768 as its recommended default parameter set, calling it a large security margin at a reasonable performance cost, and said it has no current plans to standardize additional KEMs beyond HQC. If you are picking a parameter set today and want the choice least likely to be second-guessed, that is the one.

What I would do this quarter

I build a small end-to-end encrypted messenger, and I went through this migration myself: ML-KEM-768 for key establishment and ML-DSA-65 for identity signatures, replacing a classical design. The protocol part was not the hard part. The hard part was everything around it: key storage, migration paths for existing sessions, and making sure a new key type did not silently break an old client. That experience shapes how I read this report, and it is why I think the practical checklist matters more than the dates.

First, make a cryptographic inventory. You cannot migrate what you cannot list. Walk your codebase and your infrastructure and write down every place a public key is generated, exchanged, stored, or verified. Include the boring ones: TLS certificates, code signing, backup encryption, JWT signing keys, SSH host keys. The inventory is the part the GAO found agencies missing, and it is the part most small teams skip.

Second, put ML-KEM-768 behind a flag in your key establishment path, hybrid with your existing classical exchange, and turn it on where you control both ends. Do not wait for a perfect migration plan. The hybrid validation answer means this step is safe to take now.

Third, design for crypto-agility from the start. The algorithm you pick today will not be the last one you ship. If swapping a KEM or a signature scheme requires touching twenty files and a database migration, fix that structure before you fix the algorithm. A clean seam between "what key type" and "what the protocol does with it" is worth more than picking the perfect parameter set.

Fourth, treat signatures as a separate, longer project. Key establishment can go hybrid quickly. Signatures touch trust roots, and trust roots move slowly. Start the inventory for signatures now, even if the migration itself takes years.

Fifth, watch two dates that are closer than 2030: the FAR proposed rule around December 19, 2026, and the CISA bill-of-materials elements around March 19, 2027. If you sell to anyone who sells to the government, those will show up in your contracts and questionnaires before the 2030 deadline does.

The deadline stopped being a draft

For two years, the post-quantum transition had the feel of a long-range plan: real standards, vague dates, and plenty of room to say "we will get to it." The final IR 8547 changes the texture of it. The dates are now attached to procurement rules, validation processes, and contract clauses. The deprecation scope now covers the strong classical defaults, not just the weak ones. And the agencies that were supposed to be ready first have, by the GAO's account, not finished their homework.

None of this means a quantum computer is about to break RSA next week. It means the paperwork, the procurement, and the product roadmaps have caught up with the standards. If your plan still says RSA-3072 and P-256 are fine until 2035, that plan is out of date as of October 27. Rewrite it now, while rewriting it is still cheap.

Sources: NIST IR 8547 (Final, October 2026); OMB Memorandum M-26-15 (June 2026); Executive Order 14412 (June 2026); GAO-27-108740 (October 2026); Cloud Security Alliance research note on IR 8547 (October 30, 2026).

SpecterLAN: Find the Ghosts on Your Network

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This is a submission for the Hacktoberfest Open-Source AI Challenge Week 4: Ghost in the Machine

What I Built

I built SpecterLAN, a local-first network monitor that helps you understand which devices are connected to your network and what changed between scans.

SpecterLAN can show:

  • New devices
  • Devices that went offline
  • Devices that returned
  • IP address changes
  • Devices that still need review

For the Ghost in the Machine theme, unfamiliar or forgotten devices become the "ghosts" on your network.

SpecterLAN does not assume an unknown device is dangerous. It shows what changed and lets you identify the device yourself.

It also includes Ask the Ghost, where a local AI model explains network changes using evidence collected by SpecterLAN.

Everything runs locally on your device.

Demo

Watch the SpecterLAN demo

The demo shows:

  • A baseline network scan
  • A new device appearing
  • SpecterLAN detecting the change
  • Ask the Ghost explaining the change with local AI
  • A device changing IP while keeping the same identity
  • Another device going offline

SpecterLAN also includes Demo Séance, which uses simulated network data so people can test the full experience without exposing their real network.

Code

GitHub repository:

https://github.com/rishabh-hasija/spectarlan

SpecterLAN is open source and released under the MIT License.

How I Built It

SpecterLAN is built with:

  • Python
  • Streamlit
  • SQLite
  • Ollama
  • Gemma 3 4B as the default local AI model
  • Pydantic
  • Nmap when available, with a local fallback when it is not

Network scanning happens on the user's own device.

Scan history, device names, trust labels, and other information are stored locally in SQLite.

For AI analysis, SpecterLAN sends structured evidence only to the locally running Ollama service.

The default model is gemma3:4b.

SpecterLAN also detects other compatible Ollama models already installed on the device and lets the user select one from a dropdown.

Ask the Ghost does not decide whether a device is malicious.

Instead, SpecterLAN creates evidence records, checks the evidence referenced by the model, and removes unsupported AI output.

Why Does Open Innovation Matter?

Network information is private.

A network scan can contain device names, local addresses, hardware details, and information about devices inside someone's home.

I did not want SpecterLAN to send that information to an external AI API.

Using Ollama and an open-weight model means the AI runs on the same device as SpecterLAN.

There is:

  • No cloud AI
  • No API key
  • No SpecterLAN account
  • No registration
  • No shared database
  • No network history uploaded to a SpecterLAN server

Users can also switch to another compatible local Ollama model.

Even without AI, device discovery, tracking, and scan history continue to work.

This open and local approach keeps the user's network data under their control.

The Spirit of It

Devices appear, disappear, return, and sometimes change addresses.

SpecterLAN treats those changes like apparitions on your network.

A forgotten device might suddenly appear again. A new device might show up without a recognizable name. Ask the Ghost helps explain what SpecterLAN observed.

The Halloween theme is part of the experience, while the findings remain evidence-based.

Unknown does not mean malicious.

Prize Categories

Best Use of Gemma

SpecterLAN uses Gemma 3 4B through Ollama as its default local AI model.

Gemma powers Ask the Ghost, which explains changes between saved network scans using structured local evidence.

The model runs on the user's own device, so private network information does not need to be sent to a hosted AI provider.

n8n 3.0 removes the Function node, and published workflows stop activating

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

n8n 3.0.0 shipped as a beta on 27 October, stable lands in early December, and it is an operational release rather than a feature release. Around three dozen legacy nodes go away, including Function, Function Item, Item Lists, Cron and Interval.

The dangerous sentence is in n8n's own docs: "Workflows that use a removed node fail when they run. If such a workflow is published, it doesn't activate when n8n 3.0 starts, so its triggers stop firing."

No red execution. No retry. No email. The workflow is simply not active, and you find out from a customer.

The mapping

Removed in 3.0 Use instead -------------------- ---------------------------------------------- Function Code node, Run Once for All Items Function Item Code node, Run Once for Each Item Item Lists Split Out, Aggregate, Sort, Limit, Remove Duplicates, Summarize Cron, Interval Schedule Trigger HTML Extract current node version iCalendar current node version Binary file nodes current node versions

Some migrations are automatic (the AI Transform node becomes a Code node with the same generated JavaScript). Function and Function Item are not. You replace those yourself.

Find them in ten minutes

  1. Settings, then Migration Report. Exists from 2.39 onwards, and it lists the affected workflows, nodes and settings on your own instance. Save a copy.
  2. On an older version, upgrade to 2.39 first. The report is worth one small, reversible upgrade.
  3. Count the node types yourself. Export workflows through /api/v1/workflows or the CLI and search the JSON for n8n-nodes-base.function, functionItem, itemLists, n8n-nodes-base.cron, n8n-nodes-base.interval.
  4. Write the list down with workflow name, node and purpose. That list is your upgrade plan and your rollback argument.

Fix on 2.x, not after

The Function to Code conversion is the only real work, because the execution model changes:

// Function (legacy): input is items, you return an array of items return items.map(item => ({ json: { ...item.json, seen: true } })); // Code, Run Once for All Items: read all input, return an array const items = $input.all(); return items.map(item => ({ json: { ...item.json, seen: true } }));

For Function Item, use Code with Run Once for Each Item: the input is a single item and you return that item's new data.

Test each migrated workflow on 2.x, run it once manually, check the output shape the next node expects, then publish. Then upgrade, with a backup, because 3.0 cannot be rolled back.

The check nobody does after upgrading

Open the workflow list and look at the active toggle on every workflow, not only the ones you migrated. A workflow that lost activation looks identical to a healthy one in a screenshot. The list is the only place the difference shows, and only if you look.

Full guide with the audit steps and sources: voxahq.in. No affiliate links.

The Role of Artificial Intelligence and Inkjet Printers in Smart 3D Panel Manufacturing: From Quality Control to Production Accuracy

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Introduction

The construction industry is gradually moving toward smarter and more efficient manufacturing methods. As demand for lightweight, durable, and energy-efficient building materials increases, manufacturers are looking for technologies that can improve production accuracy while reducing waste and operational costs. One product that has gained attention in modern construction is the 3D wall panel, also known as the 3D panel.

These panels typically consist of an expanded polystyrene (EPS) foam core positioned between two layers of welded steel wire mesh. Diagonal steel connectors pass through the foam core, creating a three-dimensional structure. Once installed, the panels are generally covered with concrete or shotcrete to form a strong building element.

Although the basic manufacturing process is well established, modern technologies can make it more precise and easier to manage. Artificial intelligence (AI) and industrial inkjet printers are two technologies that offer valuable opportunities in this field. AI can support production monitoring and quality inspection, while inkjet printing provides an efficient method for marking products with important manufacturing information.

Understanding how these technologies work together helps explain the potential of smart manufacturing in the 3D panel industry.

Understanding the 3D Panel Manufacturing Process

Before exploring the role of AI and inkjet printing, it is important to understand how 3D panels are manufactured.

The production process usually begins with preparing the EPS foam core. The foam is cut into specific dimensions according to the required panel design. Steel wire mesh is then positioned on both sides of the foam, and diagonal connectors are installed to connect the two mesh layers.

The final structure must meet several technical requirements, including accurate dimensions, appropriate wire spacing, reliable connections, and consistent foam density. Even small manufacturing variations can affect installation, material consumption, and the overall performance of the finished panel.

For this reason, quality control is an essential part of the production process. Manufacturers need reliable methods to monitor each stage, identify defects, and maintain consistent product specifications.

How Artificial Intelligence Improves 3D Panel Production

Artificial intelligence can help manufacturers analyze production data, recognize patterns, and identify potential problems more efficiently. Instead of relying exclusively on manual inspections, production facilities can use AI-supported systems to monitor different manufacturing stages.

1. Automated Quality Inspection

One of the most promising applications of AI is automated visual inspection. Cameras installed along production lines can capture images of finished panels and send them to computer vision systems for analysis.

AI models trained on relevant production images may identify visible issues such as:

  • Irregular foam dimensions
  • Missing or misplaced wire connections
  • Uneven wire mesh positioning
  • Damaged foam surfaces
  • Inconsistent panel geometry

These systems can help operators detect defects earlier and reduce the likelihood of defective products reaching the packaging stage.

However, AI inspection should complement, rather than replace, technical testing and established quality control procedures. Structural performance still requires appropriate engineering assessments and material testing.

2. Improving Cutting Accuracy

The EPS foam core must be cut according to specific measurements. Inaccurate cutting can create difficulties during mesh installation and lead to unnecessary material waste.

AI-based production systems can analyze cutting data and identify patterns associated with measurement errors. When integrated with suitable sensors and automated cutting equipment, these systems can support more consistent production.

For example, a smart manufacturing system may detect repeated dimensional deviations and alert operators before the problem affects a large batch of panels.

3. Predictive Maintenance for Manufacturing Equipment

Unexpected equipment failures can interrupt production and increase operational costs. Cutting machines, welding equipment, conveyors, and other production components require regular maintenance.

AI-powered predictive maintenance systems can analyze information such as machine temperature, vibration, operating speed, and production history. By recognizing unusual patterns, they may help maintenance teams identify potential equipment problems before a breakdown occurs.

This approach can improve production planning, reduce unexpected downtime, and support more stable manufacturing operations.

4. Reducing Material Waste

Efficient material consumption is important for manufacturers working with EPS foam and steel wire. Incorrect cutting, defective welding, and repeated production adjustments can increase waste.

AI can analyze production records and help identify the stages where material losses occur most frequently. Manufacturers can use these insights to adjust production settings, improve cutting plans, and reduce avoidable errors.

The result can be a more controlled manufacturing process with better resource utilization.

The Role of Industrial Inkjet Printers in 3D Panel Manufacturing

While AI focuses on data analysis and production optimization, industrial inkjet printers provide a practical solution for product identification and traceability.

During manufacturing, panels need to be identified using information that helps operators, distributors, and construction teams distinguish between different products and production batches.

Industrial inkjet printers can print variable information directly onto suitable product surfaces, labels, or packaging materials without requiring physical contact with the printing surface.

Depending on the production requirements, manufacturers can use these systems to print:

  • Production dates
  • Batch and serial numbers
  • Product codes
  • Manufacturer identification
  • Technical specifications
  • QR codes and barcodes

This information makes it easier to organize inventory, track production batches, and manage product distribution.

1. Printing Production Dates and Batch Numbers

Production date and batch identification are important for maintaining manufacturing records. When each batch receives a unique identification code, manufacturers can connect finished products to their corresponding production information.

For example, if a quality issue is discovered after delivery, the manufacturer can use the printed batch number to identify the relevant production period and investigate the manufacturing records.

An industrial inkjet printer can automate this marking process, reducing the need for manual labeling and helping maintain consistent identification across large production volumes.

2. Contactless Printing on Different Materials

One advantage of industrial inkjet technology is its ability to print without direct contact with the product surface. This can be useful when marking packaging materials or suitable surfaces that are difficult to process using traditional contact-based printing methods.

Manufacturers can select an appropriate ink and printing system according to the material, surface condition, production speed, and environmental requirements.

For 3D panels {تری دی پنل }, printing on packaging, protective films, labels, or designated marking areas may be more practical than printing directly onto the foam or steel mesh.

3. Supporting Product Traceability

Traceability allows manufacturers to follow products throughout the supply chain. A printed barcode or QR code can connect a physical panel or package to a digital record containing information such as product dimensions, production batch, manufacturing date, and inspection status.

This approach can make warehouse operations more organized and help construction companies verify product information before installation.

Combining AI and Inkjet Printing for Smart Manufacturing

The real potential of these technologies becomes clearer when they are integrated into a connected production system.

Imagine a manufacturing facility where AI-supported cameras inspect each panel after assembly. The system checks visible characteristics and sends inspection results to a production management platform. Once a panel passes the required checks, an industrial inkjet printer marks its packaging with a unique batch number or QR code.

The printed code can then connect the physical product to its digital manufacturing record.

This workflow offers several advantages:

Improved production tracking: Each batch can be associated with manufacturing and inspection information.

Faster identification: Operators can identify products without manually checking multiple documents.

Better quality management: Inspection records can help manufacturers investigate recurring defects.

More organized inventory: Product codes make warehouse classification and distribution easier.

Reduced manual operations: Automated marking and inspection can reduce repetitive tasks.

However, successful integration requires compatible software, reliable communication between equipment, suitable printing hardware, and a clearly defined data management process.

Important Factors When Choosing an Industrial Inkjet Printer

Selecting the right industrial inkjet printer depends on the specific requirements of the production facility. Not every printing system is suitable for every surface or manufacturing environment.

Several factors should be considered before purchasing a machine.

Printing Speed

The printer must operate at a speed compatible with the production line. A system that cannot keep up with manufacturing output may create bottlenecks.

Printing Surface

Manufacturers should determine whether information will be printed on packaging film, cardboard, labels, or another material. Ink compatibility and adhesion are important considerations.

Print Quality

Small production codes, barcodes, and QR codes must remain readable. The printer should provide sufficient resolution and consistent printing performance for the intended application.

Maintenance Requirements

Industrial environments may contain dust, vibration, and changing temperatures. Choosing equipment with appropriate protection, accessible maintenance components, and reliable technical support can help reduce operational interruptions.

Integration with Production Systems

For smart manufacturing, the printer should be compatible with production management software and other automation equipment where required. This makes it easier to synchronize product identification with manufacturing data.

The Future of Smart 3D Panel Manufacturing

The development of smart manufacturing is creating new opportunities for producers of construction materials. Artificial intelligence, machine vision, industrial automation, and digital identification systems can help manufacturers improve visibility across their production operations.

In the future, AI systems may become more capable of analyzing production conditions, identifying complex defects, and recommending adjustments based on historical manufacturing data. At the same time, industrial inkjet printers and digital coding systems can make product identification faster and more reliable.

These developments do not eliminate the importance of skilled operators, engineering standards, or physical quality testing. Instead, they provide additional tools that help manufacturers make better decisions and maintain more consistent production processes.

For companies producing 3D panels, investing in suitable automation technologies can be a practical step toward improving efficiency and strengthening quality management.

Conclusion

Artificial intelligence and industrial inkjet printing serve different but complementary purposes in modern 3D panel manufacturing. AI supports production analysis, automated inspection, predictive maintenance, and material optimization. Industrial inkjet printers simplify product marking by printing production dates, batch numbers, product codes, and other identification information.

When these technologies are connected through a well-designed manufacturing system, they can improve traceability, reduce manual work, and provide better control over production operations.

As construction material manufacturing continues to evolve, the combination of intelligent software and reliable industrial equipment is likely to become increasingly valuable for companies seeking greater accuracy, efficiency, and consistency.

Aggressor-Side Classification in Order-Flow Indicators: Quote Rule, Tick Rule, and Unknown Trades

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A trade print tells us that a transaction occurred at a particular price and size. It does not always tell us whether the buyer or seller initiated it. Yet footprint charts, bid/ask volume displays, and cumulative delta all depend on assigning trades to a side. A classification error made at ingestion can propagate into every downstream visualization.

There is no universally correct fallback for missing or stale quote data. The useful engineering question is not simply “Was this a buy or a sell?” It is “What evidence supports this classification, and what do we do when that evidence is incomplete?”
Aggressor side is not trade direction
Every executed trade has a buyer and a seller. “Buyer-initiated” generally means an incoming order crossed the spread to execute against resting sell liquidity; “seller-initiated” means the analogous action against resting buy liquidity. It does not mean that the buyer knows the future direction of the market or that a buy signal has appeared.

Some feeds provide an aggressor-side indicator. Others provide trades and quotes that let you infer one. Verify the feed's documented semantics before treating either as ground truth. Equities may require a consolidated best bid/offer; a futures feed may supply venue-specific inside quotes. These inputs are not interchangeable without a data-policy decision.
Method 1: The quote rule
When an appropriate bid/ask snapshot can be aligned with the trade, a common classification rule is:

  • Trade at or above the contemporaneous ask: buyer-aggressed candidate.
  • Trade at or below the contemporaneous bid: seller-aggressed candidate.
  • Trade inside the spread, or with inconsistent quotes: unknown. “Contemporaneous” does a lot of work here. Quote and trade messages may be delivered on different channels, arrive out of order, or use timestamps with different precision. Comparing a trade with the quote that happens to be most recently received can silently contaminate the result. Validate source, ordering, quote age, and whether the quote was actually applicable at execution time.

If both comparisons appear true because the market is locked or crossed, do not let branch order arbitrarily pick a winner: flag the quote as unusable under your documented policy.
Method 2: The tick rule
When reliable quote context is unavailable, the tick rule uses the change from the previous trade price:

  • Higher than the previous print → buy candidate.
  • Lower than the previous print → sell candidate.
  • Same price → apply an explicit zero-tick policy, such as retaining the previous nonzero price-change direction, or mark unknown. This is a heuristic, not a substitute for a contemporaneous quote or an exchange-provided aggressor flag. It can behave poorly when spreads are wide or the tape contains gaps or reordering. In a report or indicator settings panel, name the method rather than simply labeling the output “true buy volume.” A classification function with an honest third state The example below is standalone C#-style application logic, not a drop-in NinjaScript indicator. It illustrates a deliberately conservative quote policy, an optional tick-rule fallback, and a separate unknown outcome. enum Side { Buy, Sell, Unknown }

sealed class TradePrint
{
public double Price;
public DateTime TimeUtc;
}

sealed class Quote
{
public double Bid;
public double Ask;
public DateTime TimeUtc;
}

static Side Classify(
TradePrint trade,
Quote quote,
double? previousTradePrice,
Side previousTickDirection,
TimeSpan maxQuoteAge)
{
bool quoteUsable = quote != null
&& quote.Bid > 0
&& quote.Ask > quote.Bid
&& trade.TimeUtc >= quote.TimeUtc
&& trade.TimeUtc - quote.TimeUtc <= maxQuoteAge;

if (quoteUsable) { if (trade.Price >= quote.Ask) return Side.Buy; if (trade.Price <= quote.Bid) return Side.Sell; return Side.Unknown; // Inside spread: no forced side } if (!previousTradePrice.HasValue) return Side.Unknown; if (trade.Price > previousTradePrice.Value) return Side.Buy; if (trade.Price < previousTradePrice.Value) return Side.Sell; return previousTickDirection; // Configurable zero-tick policy

}
The time threshold is a policy input, not a universal constant. Timestamp checks cannot fix unsynchronized providers or missing quote changes. Also, previousTickDirection must be maintained from the previous nonzero price change, not accidentally overwritten with Unknown on every tied print.

A production pipeline should preserve a classification method and ideally a confidence/provenance field alongside the side label. Otherwise, a chart may combine exchange flags, quote-rule guesses, and tick-rule guesses while making them look equally certain.
What should delta do with unknown volume?
For each print, aggregate its size into exactly one of three buckets: buy, sell, or unknown. Then define your displayed delta explicitly:
classified_delta = buy_volume - sell_volume
classified_volume = buy_volume + sell_volume
all_traded_volume = classified_volume + unknown_volume
Unknown volume should not disappear from total traded volume. Conversely, forcing all unknown prints into buy or sell overstates the certainty of delta. Showing an “unclassified %” beside delta can be more honest than a falsely precise histogram.

A useful diagnostic is to compare classification rates by instrument, session, feed, and replay/live mode. A sudden rise in unknown volume may indicate a quote-availability change or timestamp alignment issue—not a change in market sentiment.
Separate ingestion, classification, aggregation, and rendering
Keep these stages independent:

  1. Ingestion: validate timestamps, ordering, trade sizes, and quote provenance.
  2. Classification: apply a selected policy and record the method used.
  3. Aggregation: map prices to integer tick indices and maintain buy/sell/unknown counters without floating-point price-key drift.
  4. Rendering: consume consistent snapshots rather than mutable accumulation buffers. This separation lets you replace a fallback method without rewriting the footprint renderer. It also makes feed comparisons testable: run the same recorded prints through two classification policies and inspect where they disagree.

For wider context on order-book, tape, and delta concepts, see the ICF Market volume and order-flow education series. The classification policy described here is an educational design example, not a claim about any proprietary ICF Market implementation.
The test I would run before shipping
Build a small replay fixture with: normal at-bid and at-ask prints, an inside-spread trade, a locked quote, a stale quote, the first print of a session, and a sequence of same-price trades. Assert that all volume remains accounted for and that the output names its source policy.

Discussion: If you have both a feed-supplied aggressor flag and the quote rule, do you treat one as authoritative, record the disagreement, or surface both?
Disclosure: This article was prepared with AI assistance. The code is illustrative and has not been tested against a live market-data feed. This is educational material, not trading advice.

"'29 is their official timeline," Kirby told podcast host Monica Langley. "It's not going to happen in '29 -- it's going to take longer." [...] Much of Boom's recent focus has been on developing natural gas turbines to power AI data centers as an additional revenue stream. On October 22, the company unveiled another factory in Centennial, Colorado, dedicated to producing a 42-megawatt natural gas turbine called Superpower for data centers. [...] Boom described its new Superpower factory as making turbine parts while still undergoing construction. It aims to deliver the first gas turbines to AI data center customers in mid-2027 while ramping up annual production to 25 turbines in 2028 and 50 turbines by 2029. [...] Still, Boom has characterized the development of a gas turbine for data centers as being synergistic with its design of the Symphony engine for the Overture airliner. During the October announcement, it also described having completed a prototype turbine core, called Sprint Core, that is intended to eventually power both the Superpower gas turbine and Symphony engine for the airliner. That turbine core is set to undergo testing at the Colorado Air & Space Port, with Boom planning to fire the turbine for the first time before Thanksgiving. That would be a concrete development milestone, but the company still has much to do in proving it can get both its AI data center and supersonic airliner ventures off the ground.
