Amazon sends chills through its workforce with mixed message memo about AI use

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There's a ridiculously simple test you can use to figure out whether AI mandates at a given company are well-considered, or are being shoved out the door by out-of-touch buffoons: What happens when an engineer documents that AI made a task worse? Are they taken seriously, or are they marginalized and eventually managed out?

Conveniently, this week Amazon published a treatise by Terk Terkowitz, a VP/Distinguished Engineer/Final Boss of Their Independent Contributor Technical Career Track, on the role of the developer in the age of AI. It was originally an internal missive sent to their software engineers and their managers back in September, which is an interesting lag for a document that sounds this confident. It also maps exceedingly well to what other organizations are struggling with.

I think it's a good document, but I can't help but view it with a somewhat cynical eye. I see it as an attempt to make the future of the profession of software engineering feel less bleak. And it's feeling mighty bleak for a lot of folks right now.

What's the context?

This treatise comes at a time of dramatic upheaval. Tech as an industry is no stranger to change (usually at 3AM, heralded by the pager), but something about the current AI-ification of the world strikes many of us as fundamentally dissimilar from previous revolutions. Folks are scared about their career prospects. "Should I give up tech and get into farming?" is a sincere question multiple people have asked me. I don't know! Tech is already a goat rodeo, so you've got a leg up if you do?

This anxiety isn't theoretical. In May, Amazon wisely shut down KiroRank, an employee-built cash incineration leaderboard that ranked people by token usage, after the obvious thing happened: people spun up junk tasks to climb the ranks. This is now called "tokenmaxxing," and the Terkowitz treatise specifically calls it out as being at odds with "frugality." Meta did the same thing with "Claudenomics" and 85K employees that same week. And Amazon reportedly announced another 250 job cuts this week. I can't tell you that these things are related. But I can tell you that every engineer who read the memo assumes that they are.

What's the thing say?

This document contains a lot of great stuff. I have a tremendous level of respect, both personal and professional, for Amazon's Principal Engineering community; I'm led to understand that that respect may not be reciprocated, but that's okay. I am, by their standards, a lapsed engineer who ran away to join the circus as one of its lesser clowns.

Terkowitz's memo emphasizes customer outcomes and engineering judgement, two things I would absolutely not delegate to AI any time soon. (Note: I am writing this in October of 2026, with the full understanding that previous comments on the capabilities where AI falls short tend to age like fine milk.) It documents work that (today) AI tools aren't improving. That's way better than assuming that every objection to AI inherently comes from some self-interested Luddite who's scared of being replaced. It also says something smart about measurement: look at the 90th percentile of AI adoption, at those teams burning the most tokens, and check whether their delivery actually went up commensurately. This is great! Shout it from the rooftops; we should all be doing such things.

But still, I wonder.

The memo says “If your work does not benefit from current AI tooling, that is a finding, not a failing.” Document it, bring it to your manager, work through the problem. I agree with every word of that! The question is whether anyone's promo doc is gonna survive citing it.

Here's what happens next. The memo concedes that input metrics are "deeply flawed," but then in the next paragraph says to go ahead and use them anyway, carefully. It then warns that non-adopters are falling behind. Those are three different positions within three sentences and they're not at all aligned with one another.

That's not muddled; I think it's cover. A manager can cite "a finding, not a failing" when a strong engineer skips the AI tool, and then cite "falling behind" when it's time to PIP someone. Both are congruent with their stated policy; you get to decide which interpretation you want to go with in a given situation.

How will managers handle justified AI non-use? Are promotion and performance decisions being made in a way that rewards these long term outcomes? Or are they instead taking the easy weasel way out, optimizing for what's cheap to measure, and rewarding tool activity? Because if you give me a metric, I will turn it into a target. So will an overwhelming majority of employees. When that happens, everyone loses — it's how KiroRank happened in the first place.

Everyone's selling tokens — including Amazon

I write this after two days of attending GitHub Universe, where they talked about how agents start roughly 40% of PRs now. And yet, Jay Parikh’s keynote argument was that useful output wasn't "more code," but rather completed outcomes: features shipped, bugs fixed, security issues addressed, loudmouth columnists silenced, etc. The problem here is that their entire business model cuts against that; they're billing in a model that charges customers on a consumption basis, not an output basis. There's nothing, today, that ties "AI spend" to "output that was worth the effort."

Which brings us back to Amazon. The memo says tokenmaxxing is at odds with frugality, and for Amazon’s internal cost line, it is. Meanwhile AWS bills Bedrock and Kiro customers by the token. Inside the building, tokenmaxxing is a problem to be stamped out. Outside it, it’s the revenue plan. ®

Introducing AUTONET - A Rust CLI That Finds the Right Local IP for Your Development Server

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ever switched between office Wi-Fi, home Wi-Fi, Ethernet, or a mobile hotspot and had to update your development server's IP address again?

That's the problem I'm trying to solve with AUTONET, an open-source, cross-platform networking tool built in Rust.

The Problem

When developing web or mobile applications, your machine can have multiple IP addresses from Wi-Fi, Ethernet, Docker, virtual machines, and VPNs.

Finding the correct address to access your local development server from another device can become frustrating.

For example, your application might be running on localhost:3000, but you want to test it from your phone.

Which IP address should you use?

What AUTONET Does

AUTONET inspects your machine's network interfaces and routing information to select a suitable IP address for local development.

Some of its capabilities include:

  • Automatic IP selection — Finds a suitable local network address.
  • Network change detection — Monitors changes between Wi-Fi, Ethernet, and other interfaces.
  • Docker and VPN awareness — Avoids selecting unsuitable virtual network addresses by default.
  • Developer integration — Provides environment variables for development applications.
  • Local hostname discovery — Supports .local hostname advertisement using mDNS.
  • QR code generation — Makes it easier to open development applications on mobile devices.
  • Network diagnostics — Helps identify potential connectivity problems.

Built with Rust

AUTONET uses a modular Rust architecture with separate components for network discovery, address selection, and CLI operations.

It uses native operating-system networking APIs to inspect interfaces and routes.

Linux support is implemented, while macOS and Windows implementations still need real-hardware validation.

Still Evolving

The project is open-source and under active development.

I'm particularly interested in feedback and contributions around:

  • Testing across Linux, macOS, and Windows
  • Network switching and connectivity edge cases
  • Improving address selection and diagnostics
  • Documentation and developer experience

If you're interested in Rust, networking, operating systems, or developer tools, feel free to explore the repository.

GitHub: https://github.com/phravins/AUTONET

I'd love to hear your thoughts on the approach.

How do you currently handle changing local IP addresses when testing applications across multiple devices?

Feedback, issues, and pull requests are always welcome!

OpenNVR 0.1.10: running an open-source NVR's AI on the Qualcomm Hexagon NPU

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

OpenNVR is an open-source (AGPL) NVR that records and streams from any ONVIF or RTSP camera and runs the AI model you choose behind them: YOLO detection, pose, face recognition, plate OCR, open-vocabulary detection, and a vision-language agent that can look at a scene and reason about it. It runs on hardware you own, air-gapped if you need, with every inference audited.

Version 0.1.10 is the first release with hardware acceleration flavours, and this post is about the one I have numbers for: Qualcomm's Hexagon NPU.

The board

Arduino Ventuno Q: Qualcomm Dragonwing IQ-8275 (QCS8275), eight ARM cores, a Hexagon NPU, Ubuntu 24.04. Small, fanless, the kind of thing that sits behind a few cameras in a shop or a plant.

The numbers

All measured on that board with the published container images. Full windows, settle times and caveats are in the benchmark doc.

Tier-0 detector (YOLO, one frame):

Device ms per frame CPU, 8 threads 164 Hexagon NPU, fp16 8.6

In the full stack (streams, decode, tracking, the API, all on the same box):

Device Cameras at 2 fps, no drops Host idle CPU 4 (10 cameras drop half their frames) ~4 % NPU 16, 0 dropped 46 %

The adapters that sit behind the detector, end-to-end on one 1080p JPEG:

Adapter CPU NPU YOLO 141 ms 77 ms Pose 108 ms 64 ms Package detection 100 ms 60 ms Face recognition (InsightFace) 443 ms 62 ms Plate OCR 248 ms 62 ms

The NPU's own share of an adapter call is about 9 ms. The rest is JPEG decode and preprocessing on a busy CPU, so the adapter path is now bounded by image handling, not inference.

How it works

Everything goes through ONNX Runtime's QNN execution provider, shipped as a plugin wheel (onnxruntime-qnn). There is no Qualcomm SDK to install on the host. The -qualcomm container gets the DSP device node, the DMA heap, and two host libraries bind-mounted, and that is it.

git clone https://github.com/open-nvr/open-nvr.git && cd open-nvr ./start.sh # OPENNVR_ACCEL=auto detects /dev/fastrpc-cdsp and picks the qualcomm flavour

OPENNVR_ACCEL=cpu|intel|qualcomm overrides the probe. A box with only a CPU gets exactly the images it got before.

Compiled graphs are cached, so the second start skips the ~6 s compile. The pipeline reports which provider ONNX Runtime really ran on, and a request for an accelerator that silently fell back to CPU shows up as requested != effective in the metrics instead of being hidden.

Things that cost a day each

  • A plugin execution provider named in the providers= list is silently ignored. It has to be attached by device with add_provider_for_devices.
  • python:slim lacks libatomic1. The HTP backend fails to load and the session falls back to CPU with no error.
  • The first "NPU" number I got (12 ms, looked great) was a stub detector after a failed session. Check the health endpoint and the degraded counter before trusting a number.
  • The Ubuntu image ships Docker Compose 2.24.6, which randomly drops a container's second network at create. The installer now checks the Compose version and repairs network membership after every up.

Measure your own hardware

python -m detect_pipeline.bench --latency 200 --providers CPUExecutionProvider python -m detect_pipeline.bench --latency 200 --providers QNNExecutionProvider:htp

It reports median, p90 and min per device, names the provider that really ran, and exits non-zero if the accelerator fell back.

Also in 0.1.10

  • Intel iGPU and NPU flavour via OpenVINO, VAAPI decode
  • Signed self-update: each release ships a manifest signed by us and verified on the box, opt-in
  • Camera tours for display boards, camera CSV/Excel import and export, bulk actions
  • The app SDK's Look and Investigate steps for writing your own camera workflows

Next

NVIDIA, desktop GPUs and Jetson. If you have a Jetson, Rockchip or Hailo box you'd lend to a benchmark, I have one board and would like more.

  • Repo: https://github.com/open-nvr/open-nvr
  • Release notes: https://github.com/open-nvr/open-nvr/releases/tag/v0.1.10
  • Hardware guide: https://github.com/open-nvr/open-nvr/blob/main/docs/HARDWARE.md
  • Discord: https://opennvr.org/discord

Transformer Architecture in 5 Questions Developers Actually Ask (with 12 Lines of PyTorch)

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Most Transformer explainers start with the famous diagram from "Attention Is All You Need" and lose half their readers at the first box. As a developer, I found it easier to learn the architecture by answering the five questions I kept asking myself, and by writing the core operation in code.

1. What problem was the Transformer actually solving?

RNNs and LSTMs read a sentence one token at a time, like a message passed down a line of people. That makes long-range relationships fragile and training hard to parallelise. The Transformer replaced the chain with attention: every token can look directly at every other token in one step. Think of it as moving from a relay race to a meeting room where everyone can hear everyone.

2. What are Q, K and V, really?

Each token is projected into three vectors by learned weight matrices:

  • Query: what this token is looking for
  • Key: what this token offers
  • Value: the information it passes on if it turns out to be relevant

Compare queries with keys, turn the scores into weights, and use the weights to mix values. That is the whole idea.

3. What does that look like in code?

Here is scaled dot-product attention, the heart of every Transformer block:

import torch import torch.nn.functional as F def attention(q, k, v, mask=None): # q, k, v: (batch, seq_len, d_k) d_k = q.size(-1) scores = q @ k.transpose(-2, -1) / d_k ** 0.5 # compare + scale if mask is not None: scores = scores.masked_fill(mask == 0, float("-inf")) weights = F.softmax(scores, dim=-1) # normalise return weights @ v # collect values

The / d_k ** 0.5 line is easy to skip, but it stops large dot products from pushing softmax into near one-hot outputs, which would kill gradients.

4. Why multiple heads?

One attention pattern can't capture everything in "Aman told Ravi that he would lead the project": who "he" is, which verb goes with which noun, what "lead" applies to. Multi-head attention runs several attention operations in parallel on different learned projections and concatenates the results. Each head is like a different camera angle on the same match.

5. How does a model like GPT generate text with this?

Two more pieces make it work:

  • Positional encoding: attention by itself ignores order, so "dog bites man" and "man bites dog" would look identical without it.
  • Causal masking: in a decoder, the mask argument above blocks each token from attending to future tokens. The model predicts one token, appends it, and repeats.

Add a feed-forward layer, residual connections and layer normalisation around each sublayer, stack the block N times, and you have the architecture behind modern LLMs.

A quick map of the model families

Family Example Best at Encoder-only BERT Understanding: classification, search Decoder-only GPT Generation Encoder-decoder T5, original Transformer Translation, summarisation

Go deeper

I wrote a longer, beginner-friendly walkthrough that covers every block (encoder, decoder, cross-attention, residuals, masking) with everyday analogies: Transformer Architecture Explained on AheadMint.

If you've got a clearer mental model for Q/K/V, share it in the comments. I'm always collecting better analogies.

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

When choosing a coding bootcamp in India, the top three names that usually come up are Masai School, Scaler, and Geekster.

As a developer, I wanted to strip away the marketing fluff and just look at the raw data: Tech Stack, Fees, and Placement Models.

Here is a quick, factual comparison of their Full-Stack programs for 2026:

Feature Masai School Scaler Academy Geekster Primary Tech Stack MERN (MongoDB, Express, React, Node) Java, Spring Boot, CS Fundamentals MERN / Java Full Stack Duration 30-35 Weeks 7-11 Months 6-8 Months Pricing Model Pay After Placement (PAP) Upfront / EMI Pay After Placement (PAP) Minimum Placement ₹5 LPA minimum Varies by track ₹5 LPA minimum Best For Beginners needing strict structure Developers wanting deep DSA/System Design Beginners wanting MERN & strong placement support

Quick Verdict:

  • Choose Scaler if you already know basic coding and want to crack product-based giants (heavy DSA focus).

  • Choose Geekster if you want a slightly more flexible Pay-After-Placement route with a strong focus on MERN stack and interview prep.

MermaidStudio: As a PM, I Built a Native Mac Mermaid Editor to Fix My AI-to-Client Workflow

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As a Product Manager, I live in flowcharts, sequence diagrams, and user journeys. Over the past year, my workflow for creating diagrams has evolved into a heavy collaboration with AI: I brainstorm product logic with AI, have it generate .md files with Mermaid.js syntax, and then I need to review, tweak, and send clean diagram images to clients and stakeholders for confirmation.

Sounds simple, right? Yet every single day, the "last mile" of this workflow frustrated me.

The Friction in Existing Mermaid Editors

When you need to send 10 different flowcharts to a client in a single afternoon, existing tools start getting in your way:

  1. Too many clicks just to share an image: In web editors like mermaid.live, exporting a clean image or copying it to the clipboard takes multiple menu clicks every single time.
  2. Cropped or blurry exports: In many Markdown previewers, taking a snapshot only captures the current viewport—if your diagram is zoomed in or too wide, edges get cut off.
  3. Disconnected from local AI .md files & finalized archives: Sometimes I just want to quickly preview a temporary .md file generated by AI in a project folder; other times, once a diagram is finalized and approved by a client, I want it archived cleanly in a central library synced to my iPad—not buried in random local folders.

I wanted a native tool built specifically for this speed and structure. Since I couldn't find an editor that felt right, I built my own: MermaidStudio.

How MermaidStudio Speeds Up My Daily PM & AI Workflow

I designed MermaidStudio as a 100% native Apple ecosystem app (built with SwiftUI) around two core workflows:

1. Custom Keyboard Shortcuts for Instant Export & "Copy as Image" ⚡️

This is the feature I use dozens of times a day. When a client asks for a quick logic adjustment during a meeting, I don't want to fumble through export menus.

In MermaidStudio, you can bind custom keyboard shortcuts directly to:

  • Copy as Image — Renders the complete, high-resolution diagram straight to your system clipboard. Just hit your shortcut and press ⌘ + V in Slack, Teams, WeChat, Email, or Keynote!
  • Export as PNG / PDF / HTML — Direct one-key file export.
  • Global Hotkey — Summon MermaidStudio instantly from anywhere on your Mac.

Best of all, the export engine always calculates the full SVG bounding box at 100% scale. Even if your preview pane is zoomed in at 400% while inspecting a tiny node, hitting your Copy as Image shortcut always copies the entire, uncropped, high-DPI diagram.

2. A Two-Stage File Workflow: External AI Drafts vs. Built-in iCloud Library 🗂️

As a PM, I realized not all diagrams should live in the same place. MermaidStudio supports a dual workflow that matches how projects actually happen:

  • Stage 1 — Fast Iteration with External Files (.md / .mmd): When I'm actively iterating on a PRD or system design with AI tools (like Claude, Cursor, or ChatGPT) inside a local folder, I open the external .md, .markdown, or .mmd files directly in place. I can visually inspect the diagram, save right back to disk, and fire off quick snapshots to clients.
  • Stage 2 — Finalized Specs & Templates in the Built-in Library: Once a diagram is signed off by the client—or when I create reusable workflow templates (like standard auth flows or payment sequences)—I save a clean copy into MermaidStudio’s internal three-column Library (organized into project/client Folders, Inbox, Recent, and Trash).
  • Always in Your Pocket via iCloud: Because the built-in library syncs automatically across Mac, iPad, and iPhone via iCloud, all my finalized client diagrams are always with me. I can walk into a meeting with just my iPad or iPhone, pull up any approved architecture diagram in full-screen mode, and zoom in with zero lag.

Run It Twice, Charge Once: Idempotency Keys in n8n

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This is part of my build-in-public series on running LLM workflows in n8n in production. Everything here is from real templates I sell and support — the mistakes are mine, the fixes are tested.

The customer got charged twice, and every log said "success"

A workflow sent a payment, then a message, then a record update. One night the provider call timed out — not failed, timed out. n8n did the sensible thing and retried. The retry succeeded. So did the first attempt, it turned out: the timeout was on the way back, not on the way in. The customer was charged twice, messaged twice, and every log said "success," because each execution had, in isolation, succeeded.

Retries fix "not yet" failures. They create duplicate side effects. Any workflow that retries — and any workflow that replays from a dead-letter queue, or resumes from a checkpoint — will eventually run the same side effect twice. If that side effect is sending a message, charging a card, or creating a record, "eventually" is a bug report, not a theory.

An idempotency key is a promise about the second run

The fix is one extra field, carried through the whole workflow: an idempotency key. It identifies the intended operation, not the attempt. The same key on a second attempt must produce the same result as the first — without doing the work again.

The key has to be stable across retries, replays, and restarts. That rules out anything generated inside the attempt: a fresh UUID per run is a new operation every time, which is exactly what you are trying to prevent. The key comes from the job itself: the job id plus the operation name is the usual shape — job-4821:charge, job-4821:notify. Same job, same operation, same key, no matter how many times it runs.

Check the key before the side effect, not after

Ordering is the whole mechanism, and it is easy to get backwards. The workflow must look up the key before it performs the side effect, not log it afterwards:

  1. Before the charge, the send, or the create, check a small store for this key.
  2. If a completed result exists for the key, return that result and skip the side effect entirely. The retry becomes a no-op that reports the original outcome.
  3. If no result exists, perform the side effect, then store the result under the key — and the store write must be part of the same step, not a best-effort log line afterwards.

Step 3 is where most hand-rolled versions break. If the workflow charges the card and then crashes before recording the key, the retry sees no key and charges again. The record has to be written as close to the side effect as the system allows, and the window between them is the window where duplicates still live. Where the provider offers a native idempotency key — Stripe is the classic example — use it: the provider's store is atomic in a way your workflow's two-step version is not, and passing your key through means the provider itself dedupes the second call.

The store does not need to be fancy. A table with the key, the operation, the result, and a timestamp is enough. What it must be is durable and shared: not execution-local state, not a variable that dies with the run, and visible to every retry path — the ordinary retry, the DLQ replay, and the checkpoint resume. A key that only one retry path checks is a key that the other paths will ignore.

Keys expire, results do not vanish silently

Two practical details. First, keys need a retention policy. Keep them at least as long as your longest retry and replay window — if a DLQ entry can be replayed a week later, a key that expired after a day will not protect it. Second, a stored result is a decision, not just a cache entry: when a retry returns a stored "already charged" result, that outcome should be visible in the job record, so a human reading the run can tell "skipped as duplicate" from "never ran." A silent no-op looks identical to a skipped step in the logs, and the next person debugging will not know which one happened.

This is a different job from the failure classification in the poison-message piece and the retry budget in the backoff piece. Classification decides whether to retry at all; the retry budget decides how many times. Idempotency decides what the second attempt is allowed to do. All three have to hold: a workflow that retries without idempotency will eventually double-charge; a workflow with idempotency but no classification will retry poison inputs forever, harmlessly but pointlessly.

The test is a double delivery, not a happy path

Testing this means deliberately delivering the same job twice and asserting three things: the side effect happened exactly once, the second run returned the first run's result, and the job record shows the second run as a duplicate skip rather than a fresh success. The test I actually run is boring and specific: one job, two deliveries — one of them forced by killing the run between the side effect and the record, if the system allows it — and a count of side effects at the end. Anything more than one means the key is not being checked where it matters.

The principle

A retry is a second attempt at the same operation, and the system has to treat it that knows that. Give every side effect a key derived from the job and the operation, check it before acting, store the result with it, and let the provider dedupe where it can. The workflow gets slightly slower — one lookup per side effect — and in return a timeout stops being a double charge. When a run is retried, and it will be, the question should never be "did we already do this?" The key should already have answered it.

I'm building AI Automation Lab — production-ready n8n workflow templates. The free AI Hook Generator shows how I structure real workflows; my open-source templates live at github.com/zhp910318/n8n-ai-automation-lab.

The Lofi Girl headphones are actually real now — heres how to preorder them

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This is a safe space to admit that we've all wished we could steal the Lofi Girl's cozy desk space from her.

Well now, you kind of can. The internet icon who has given many of us company through late study sessions and long workdays is now giving us the chance to capture her impeccable cozy vibes via her iconic headphones. The Lofi Pro headphones, sold by Lofi Merch (the team behind the YouTube channel), are officially available for preorder for $225. Preorders run through Nov. 14, with the Lofi team recommending you order by the final preorder date for arrival by Christmas.

Opens in a new window Credit: Lofi Girl Lofi Pro headphones $225 at Lofi Girl
  Preorder at Lofi Girl

The headphones are basically an exact replica of Lofi Girl's (or Jade's, according to the headphones' product page). They feature the beige, red, and black color scheme, the scallop edges, and even have magnetic googly eyes, so you can decide whether or not you want to match Jade exactly. The synthetic leather ear cushions are also magnetic and can be changed out for velvet ear cushions, which are available for purchase separately on the Lofi Merch website for $24.99.

Opens in a new window Credit: Lofi Girl Lofi Girl velvet earpads $24.99 at Lofi Girl
  Preorder at Lofi Girl

Also excluded from base headphones kit is a carrying case, so if you're looking to take the headphones on the go, it's worth noting that a case will run you an additional $39.99. The headphones themselves, however, do come with a 3.5mm audio jack, active noise cancellation of up to 30 decibels, 48 hours of battery with ANC on (and 65 hours with it off), wear detection, physical on-ear controls, and multipoint pairing.

In a headphones market where more and more brands are releasing mid-range models, the Lofi Pro headphones could be a major contender with an especially unique appeal. At the very least, I'll have to get my hands on them and live out my lofi dreams to know for sure.

Review: The new Kindle Colorsoft is... fine.

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The best Kindle e-readers are essential for book lovers, and Amazon just released a new Kindle Colorsoft (and the Kindle Colorsoft Signature Edition with an aluminum body), along with a new basic Kindle, Kindle Paperwhite/Paperwhite Signature Edition, and the Kindle Click page-turner. Following last year's refresh of the Kindle Scribe lineup, that means Amazon has an all-new Kindle family, just in time for the holidays.

I've been reviewing the Kindle Colorsoft Signature Edition for Mashable for the past few weeks. Except for the new aluminum body, fig colorway, and higher storage, it's functionally the same as the base Colorsoft model, so we won't be reviewing them separately.

If you've been curious about Amazon's color e-readers, then I'd recommend reading my Kindle Colorsoft review first. Unfortunately, while there's a lot that I do love about this device, it doesn't fix the biggest problem — the color is still very disappointing.

That cover is definitely yellow-ish. Credit: Timothy Werth / Mashable

Kindle Colorsoft review: Manage your expectations when it comes to color

Now, I want to be clear about one thing: Amazon uses E Ink technology for its e-readers. This distinctive, paper-like digital display is a product of the E Ink Corporation. And the truth is that E Ink just doesn't do color very well. So, the technology is what it is.

The E Ink color experience on the Kindle Colorsoft is disappointing. That was true with the original model from 2024, and it hasn't changed with the 2026 model. What has changed is that the Colorsoft now offers true edge-to-edge color, so there are no dimming or lighting issues around the edges.

But Amazon promises readers something more: "Paper-like color perfect for comics, graphic novels, and more." And that's not what I'm seeing. In fact, some of Amazon's official photography with the Kindle Colorsoft appears to exaggerate the color saturation, at least to my eye. I've never seen color quite that bright in practice.

I haven't seen colors quite this bright on my review unit, sadly. Credit: Amazon

I put the Kindle side-by-side with illustrated books, comic books, and graphic novels I have on my bookshelf, and the difference is very obvious, even when you crank up the vividness and brightness to the limit. There's just no comparison, really.

Even in comics with more neutral tones and softer colors like the classic Batman comic The Long Halloween, the difference stands out clearly. How important is the color problem? Well, the word "color" is right there in the name — Kindle Colorsoft. Though, I suppose the emphasis should be on "soft."

The color difference is obvious. Credit: Timothy Werth / Mashable What elder millennial nerd doesn't own a copy of 'Watchmen'? Credit: Timothy Werth / Mashable

I have seen more vibrant and colorful electronic paper technology. Samsung's ePaper does a better job with color, though there are other trade-offs. It takes time for the screen to change to a new image, which isn't an option in an e-reader that demands near-instant page turns.

In my photos of Samsung's ePaper, you'll notice brighter colors, but there's a reason Samsung reserves this tech for commercial use.

Samsung's ePaper commercial display technology on display. Credit: Timothy Werth / Mashable A closer look at Samsung's ePaper commercial display technology. Credit: Timothy Werth / Mashable

If you want to read comics, magazines, or illustrated books in digital form, a tablet like the new iPad Mini or Amazon Alexa tablets would be a better option. (Mashable is in the process of reviewing both of those devices.)

And you can always go to an actual bookstore.

Amazon Kindle Colorsoft Signature Edition: What I liked

To be clear, there is a lot to love about the Kindle Colorsoft, particularly the Kindle Colorsoft Signature Edition. I love the fig color, which is a close match for the new burgundy iPhone 18 Pro. (Fun fact: An Amazon spokesperson told Mashable the fig color was inspired by the same industry trend report that inspired the burgundy iPhone.)

The new aluminum body available in the Paperwhite SE and Colorsoft SE is worth the upgrade. They have a similar feel to the Apple iPad Air, which I also loved. It's also satisfying to see the cover screen artwork and your book covers in color.

Credit: Timothy Werth / Mashable Credit: Timothy Werth / Mashable

I also appreciated the ability to zoom in on comic panels and illustrations, which is a great way to appreciate fine details in the artwork.

Credit: Timothy Werth / Mashable ...enhance! Credit: Timothy Werth / Mashable

You can double-click to read comics panel-by-panel on the Colorsoft, rather than page-by-page. Often, this works really well, but for artists who use complicated panels of varying sizes and styles (like you'll find in The Long Halloween), some of the panels fit very awkwardly on the screen.

The new Kindle Colorsoft also has a lot of the features we loved in other Kindle devices — a waterproof design, a battery that lasts for weeks, auto-adjusting light. Amazon also moved the power button to the side, which is a smart update. Only the Signature Edition supports wireless charging, and it now has magnetic pogo pins on the back. That means if you're used to using Qi wireless charging accessories with your Kindle, they won't work here. For me, this isn't a problem.

To sum up — the Kindle Colorsoft isn't a bad color e-reader. It just suffers from the same problems all color e-readers do.

The Kindle Colorsoft: Who it's for

If you believe some color is better than no color, or if you're a note-taker who loves to highlight their books, then the Kindle Colorsoft would be a smart upgrade from the basic Kindle. (Pro tip: We named the Kindle Scribe Colorsoft a Mashable Choice product, and the ability to write on the screen lets you use color in much better ways.) However, the Kobo Libra Colour is retaining its top spot as our favorite color e-reader.

The Kindle Colorsoft would also be a smart option for kids in the process of graduating from illustrated kids' books to chapter books. The Colorsoft could help make this transition a bit more inviting, and parents can also get a Kindle Colorsoft Kids Edition for $299.99.

Opens in a new window Credit: Amazon Amazon Kindle Colorsoft Signature Edition $319.99 at Amazon
  Shop Now at Amazon Opens in a new window Credit: Amazon Kindle Colorsoft Kids Edition $299.99 at Amazon
  Shop Now at Amazon

Best Sunrise Alarm Clock (2026): Hatch, Lumie

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
I’ve never been a morning person. This bedside gadget is the only thing that’s ever changed that.

I tested all of Amazons e-readers, and this is the best Kindle to buy

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Choosing a Kindle is just the start. Once you have a Kindle, there's so much you can do to make the most of it. For avid readers, Stuff Your Kindle Day and Libby let you cross every last item off your annual reading list. You can even buy Kindle accessories like remote page-turners and gooseneck stands so you don't have to hold your Kindle while reading. Which, by the way, we love Amazon's new Kindle Click and Page Turn Cover.

So, after extensive testing, reading so many books, and attending BookCon, I created this guide to the best Kindles to buy in 2026.

Meet the new 2026 Kindles

On Oct. 1, Amazon announced a redesign of the entire Kindle collection. Before the launch, Mashable's tech editor, Timothy Beck Werth, went hands-on with the devices, seeing their vivid new colors like ube, as well as the new aluminum casings on some of the e-readers. Here are all the new Kindle e-readers:

  • Kindle (16GB) — $149.99 to $169.99

  • Kindle, Aluminum (32GB) — $189.99

  • Kindle Paperwhite (16GB) — $199.99 to $219.99

  • Kindle Paperwhite Signature Edition (32GB) — $249.99

  • Kindle Colorsoft (16GB) — $289.99

  • Kindle Colorsoft Signature Edition (32GB) — $319.99

  • Kindle Kids (16GB) — $179.99

  • Kindle Kids Paperwhite (16GB) — $229.99

  • Kindle Kids Colorsoft (16GB) — $299.99

  • Kindle Click page turner — $34.99

  • Kindle Page Turn Cover — $79.99

2026 Kindle price increases

For years, Kindle prices have stayed stable, but in August 2026, they finally got an increase. Every model except the Kindle Scribe received a price increase. We can thank RAMageddon, as an Amazon spokesperson said that the increased prices are a result of "significant increases in memory and storage component costs." Here's a breakdown of all the new Kindle prices

More 2026 Kindle updates

As of May 20, 2026, Amazon no longer lend software updates or provide web services to devices released in 2012 or earlier, essentially bricking them. Users will still be able to read currently downloaded books on the device, but to add new books, they will need to connect the device to a computer. This is a huge hit, especially to Libby users who need web service access to read borrowed books.

The affected Kindles include:

  • Kindle (1st generation)

  • Kindle (2nd generation)

  • Kindle DX

  • Kindle DX Graphite

  • Kindle Keyboard

  • Kindle 4

  • Kindle Touch

  • Kindle 5

  • Kindle Paperwhite (1st generation)

Owners of these Kindle models are obviously upset, as it means they'll need to purchase a new device to maintain access to all features. While some are considering switching to a different e-reader, like a Kobo, Amazon still makes great e-readers, and it's a testament to the brand that devices that are at least 14 years old are still going strong.

Which Kindle is right for you?

If you're concerned about buying the newest tech, that's not an issue since, as of October 2026, there are nine new models to shop.

Before you choose a Kindle, you need to ask one question first: Do you want it to be waterproof? If you prefer reading near a body of water — whether in the bath, by the pool, or at the beach — that immediately narrows down your options. Since the Kindle Oasis is no longer available, your waterproof options are the Kindle Paperwhite, Paperwhite Signature Edition, Colorsoft Signature Edition, and Paperwhite Kids.

Trying to decide which Kindle is right for you? Above all, you need to decide if you need a waterproof e-reader. Credit: Samantha Mangino / Mashable

Beyond water resistance, here are a few other features to consider:

  • Display size: The basic Kindle has a 6-inch display, while the largest has a 10.2-inch display.

  • Storage capacity: Kindles range from 16GB to 32GB of storage capacity.

  • Battery life: The new Kindles generally have outstanding batteries. On the low end, they last up to six weeks per charge, while others last up to 12 weeks.

  • Writing capability: The Kindle Scribe is the only model that allows you to write with the included stylus. 

The latest Kindles all boast a maximum brightness of 94 nits and a 300 ppi, high-resolution display with zero glare, so you can enjoy sharp text and images at any viewing angle. Kindles are also equipped with WiFi connectivity, allowing you to download all the reading material you could ever want. Plus, you can listen to audiobooks on Audible via the built-in Bluetooth connection.

Not to mention, out of all the e-readers I've tested, Kindles have the best compatibility with Libby. I love the digital platform because I can read for free and support my local library. Some other e-readers require you to download your library e-book to a computer and then upload it to an e-reader, but with Kindles, your e-books are sent straight to your device with a click.

And if you were a die-hard Kindle Oasis fan and are attached to page-turning buttons, your best option is to get the Kindle Paperwhite Signature Edition with the Page Turn Cover.

SEE ALSO: I read over 50 books a year. Here are my favorite e-readers.

How to get free e-books on Kindle

With just a click, transfer your Libby e-book to your Kindle device. Credit: Samantha Mangino / Mashable Your Libby ebook will automatically populate in your Kindle library. Credit: Samantha Mangino / Mashable

The best way to read free e-books on a Kindle is with Libby. Libby allows you to borrow e-books through libraries, and all you need is a library card. Through the Libby app, borrow books and then select the "Read with Kindle" option, which allows you to send these e-books to your Kindle library.

Another option for finding free e-books is through Stuff Your Kindle events. These are author-driven events that offer limited-time deals on e-books, often making them heavily discounted or totally free.

How to make the most of your Kindle

Through our testing and daily use, we've spent a lot of time with Kindles, learning how to make the most of them. We've written extensively on all the ways to maximize your Kindle, so be sure to browse through our guides and explainers:

The 10 best e-readers weve ever tested — including the 2026 Kindles

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

So, what is the best e-reader for 2026? I'm regularly testing new e-readers (and tablets) and cycling through different models to stay up to date on the best options available. If you're gearing up to read more books this year, I can recommend a model you'll love.

But what's the e-reader? Well, Amazon just released my new favorite e-reader.

Featured Video For You

Latest e-reader news

  • On Oct. 1, Amazon dropped a redesigned collection of Kindles. We've tested the latest e-readers and named the Kindle Paperwhite Signature Edition the best overall e-reader.

  • As of September 2026, we've added the Boox Go 6 to this guide, naming it the best Kindle alternative. We also named the Kindle Paperwhite the best e-reader for the beach (in addition to being the best overall) and named the Durobo Krono the best e-reader for audiobooks.

  • In August 2026, Amazon increased the price of Kindles. Now the basic Kindle costs $149.99 ($40 increase) and the Kindle Paperwhite costs $199.99 ($40 increase).

  • In July 2026, Boox announced it was releasing a pocket-sized e-reader, the Picco. After IFA 2026, rumors floated that the Boox Picco would be released in November 2026.

  • Also in July 2026, Xteink dropped the X4 Pro, a new tiny e-reader with a touchscreen.

  • As of April 2026, we have included the Durobo Krono and the Xetink X4 e-reader in this guide.

  • In May 2026, Amazon ceased support for e-readers released in 2012 or earlier, which includes the first generation of Kindle and Kindle Paperwhite.

  • Also in May 2026, Kobo announced its integration with Storygraph, which syncs your on-device reading progress with the Storygraph app.

Which are the most affordable e-readers?

When picking an e-reader, price is always a consideration. However, almost all e-readers fall into the same price range. Kindle and Kobo e-readers both start around $149.99/$159.99, while the tiny Boox Picco is just $109.99. If you want handwriting capabilities, Kobo and Boox make the most affordable options, as Kindle's Scribes start at $500.

Other e-readers we've tested

From left to right: Kindle Paperwhite, Nook GlowLight 4 Plus, Kindle Scribe Credit: Samantha Mangino / Mashable

  • Remarkable PaperPro: Whenever you search for e-readers, Remarkable's tablets will always pop up in the search results, but unfortunately, it's hard to obtain ePUB files on the Remarkable. With Remarkable, you can access files within Google Drive, Dropbox, and OneDrive. If you have any locked ePUB files — like from Libby — you won't be able to access them on a Remarkable tablet. So, after testing the Remarkable tablet myself, it proved to be a better document reader or digital journal than an e-reader.

  • Onyx Boox Go 7: While we love the Boox Go 6, we didn't love the Go 7. It's the same size as the Kobo Libra or Amazon Kindle, but it really lags on processing speed. Reading ePUB files from Libby required downloading the Adobe Digital Editions app onto the Boox Go 7, and there was significant lag when opening and closing the app. While it was an easier process than getting library books on a Nook, it was still a major pain compared to Kindle or Kobo's integrations. The Boox Go 7 lagged and took ages to load files. I was most looking forward to testing this Boox because of its page-turning buttons, but unfortunately, I found that they didn't work on my device.

  • Boox Palma 2: I loved reading on the Boox Palma. It's a really solid palm-sized e-reader with an open Android system, so you can download any reading app from the Google Play store, including Libby, Kindle, and Kobo. Unfortunately, the Palma 2 is being discontinued, and its availability is dwindling, which is why it's not included in this list.

Where's the Nook on this list?

In terms of big-name e-readers, you might notice one omission from this list: the Barnes & Noble Nook. I have tested this e-reader, and it was previously featured on this list. However, after testing more devices, it no longer makes the cut. In comparison to any of the Kindles or the Kobo Libra, the Nook has sloth-like performance, making it frustrating to use.

Plus, getting my library books onto the Nook was a nightmare, as it was a multi-step process that required downloading to a computer before uploading to a Nook. Its only redeeming quality was the buttons on either side of the device, which made page turning a breeze. However, unless you are a loyal buyer of Barnes & Noble books, I recommend steering clear of the Nook.

Where to get e-reader books for free

Once you get an e-reader, it's time to fill it up with books, and that doesn't necessarily mean spending money. In fact, you can load up your e-reader without spending a dime; you just need to be a bit resourceful.

The best way to read nearly any book — a classic or hot new release — is with Libby. I have read hundreds of free books with the Libby app — I wouldn't have been able to test for this guide without it. It connects with your library card, allowing you to borrow from your local library's digital collection. Plus, both Kobo and Kindle have exceptional Libby integrations.

Another way to build your library is through Stuff Your Kindle Day events. These are 24-hour opportunities led by authors that make books discounted or free.

If you're a fan of the classics or looking to discover a treasure lost to time, an online resource like Project Gutenberg has more than 75,000 books in the public domain that you can download and read on an e-reader.

One League, Two Sponsorship Rulebooks

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Lazio’s Polymarket shirt deal didn’t survive the summer. In August, the Rome club parted with the prediction-market company after Italian regulators blocked its website. Yet Serie A had signed its own Polymarket agreement only months earlier, and that partnership was aimed squarely at the United States.

The same company could be ruled out on a Lazio shirt in Italy while appearing in the league’s American media. The difference lies in where each deal is meant to operate.

Lazio’s Search for a Shirt Sponsor REGGIO NELL’EMILIA, ITALY – OCTOBER 27: Tijjani Noslin of S.S. Lazio celebrates scoring his team’s fifth goal during the Serie A match between US Sassuolo and SS Lazio at Mapei Stadium – Citta’ del Tricolore on October 27, 2026 in Reggio nell’Emilia, Italy. (Photo by Emmanuele Ciancaglini/Getty Images)

Lazio agreed a two-season sponsorship with Polymarket in April 2026, reportedly worth around €19 million, with an option for another year. It was a significant deal for a club that had struggled to fill the front of its shirt.

A March breakdown of Serie A shirt sponsorship earnings identified Lazio as the only club in the division without a main shirt partner. Inter and Milan, by comparison, were each receiving around €30 million annually from their respective arrangements.

By July, the optimism had faded. Italy’s Customs and Monopolies Agency, known as ADM, had again blocked access to the platform, treating it as an unauthorised gambling operator. Keeping its branding on Lazio shirts had become difficult to reconcile with the Italian advertising rules.

Why an American Deal Is Different FLORENCE, ITALY – NOVEMBER 22: General view inside the stadium Artemio Franchi during the Serie A match between ACF Fiorentina and Juventus FC at Artemio Franchi on November 22, 2025 in Florence, Italy. (Photo by Gabriele Maltinti/Getty Images)

Italy’s Dignity Decree restricts advertising for gambling and betting with cash winnings, including sports sponsorship. Whether a prediction market belongs in that category has been central to the Polymarket dispute. The company’s description of its product as a prediction exchange didn’t prevent ADM from treating its Italian activities as unauthorised gambling.

The United States has a separate regulatory system, and access to prediction markets can depend on the state and the product offered. Readers can see how these distinctions work in practice at CBS Sports Polymarket review, which outlines US eligibility requirements, location restrictions and promotional terms. Those conditions explain who may use the American platform, not whether Polymarket’s branding is permitted on a Serie A shirt in Italy.

Italian clubs need more than assurances about a sponsor’s US operations. They also need to know whether the branding they sell is permitted at home.

Serie A’s US Partnership Has Its Own Terms

On 14 May 2026, Serie A USA named Polymarket its exclusive prediction-market partner in the United States under a multi-year arrangement. According to the league’s official announcement, the agreement covers US media and digital branding, alongside prediction markets using official Serie A data supplied through Genius Sports.

Those are quite different commercial rights from putting a company logo on a Lazio shirt worn in Italian domestic competition. The league’s deal was expressly regional, aimed at American supporters rather than Italian customers.

The agreements moved in different directions over the summer:

  • 14 May: Serie A USA announced its American partnership with Polymarket.
  • July: Italian restrictions again put Lazio’s shirt deal in doubt.
  • 11 August: Lazio and Polymarket agreed to end their sponsorship.

Lazio’s announcement concerned the club’s contract. It didn’t say that Serie A USA’s separate agreement had been cancelled.

Lazio Secured Payment Despite the Split Lazio President Claudio Lotito (Photo by Marco Rosi – SS Lazio/Getty Images)

There was an important financial detail in the 11 August settlement. Lazio said Polymarket would pay the full amount stipulated for the 2026/27 season, even though the sponsorship was ending early. Reports valued that season’s portion at approximately €9.5 million.

That protects one season’s fee, but the shirt space still needs a new buyer for future campaigns. The club and Polymarket also left open the possibility of working together again if the regulatory position changes.

A Contract Must Work Where the Football Is Played

Serie A clubs are looking beyond Italy for new commercial income, particularly in the American market. Regional media partnerships allow the league to reach that audience without necessarily placing the same branding in Italian stadiums.

Lazio’s experience shows why those distinctions need to be written into agreements from the outset. A sponsor can have an international profile and still encounter a local restriction serious enough to end a shirt deal. The league may continue to pursue US partnerships, but clubs selling sponsorship space in Italy have to account for the rules governing that space.

Backbone of a beast – charting Nvidia's rise to networking titan

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia is more than just a leader in graphics and AI hardware. It has also become a titan of the networking industry with bigger datacenter Ethernet sales than any other vendor.

In Q2, Nvidia's networking revenues topped $17.5 billion. To put that in perspective, Cisco's revenue during that same period, across all products, was $17.3 billion.

GPUzilla's arrival as a networking power feels sudden, yet Jensen Huang started working towards this status in 2019 with the $6.9 billion acquisition of long-time networking partner Mellanox .

At the time, Mellanox wasn't a big name in Ethernet switching. The company was mostly known for its ConnectX NICs and InfiniBand switches. Outside of high-performance computing (HPC) and supercomputing clusters, and a big deployment at Microsoft, InfiniBand wasn't widely deployed in hyperscale datacenters.

Buying Mellanox allowed Nvidia to reposition itself as a system builder, which means something different in the context of distributed compute. Nvidia understood its datacenter customers weren't interested in buying GPUs or even servers; they wanted clusters of systems. For that, Nvidia needed interconnect and Mellanox's InfiniBand fit the bill.

Relentless co-design

Over the years, Huang has talked about co-design – designing components and software to work together for specific set of jobs rather than to be the best GPU, switch, or NIC. Its work with Mellanox is a prime example of that approach.

According to Dell'Oro Group analyst Sameh Boujelbene, Nvidia's decision to integrate compute and networking became key to the company's success.

"Customers choose Nvidia for its tightly integrated compute and networking architecture, optimized for AI workloads," she told The Register. That integration makes deployments easier and speeds time to market.

Ben Mabey, CTO of pioneering AI and machine learning company Recursion, told The Reg Nvidia's integration schemes won him over.

Founded in 2013, nearly a decade before ChatGPT made AI the subject of dinner-table discussion, Recursion sought to apply machine learning running atop GPUs to the problem of drug discovery.

This work involved churning through massive datasets which, Mabey tells us, now total more than 50 petabytes in size.

"When you think about our workloads, it makes LLMs workload training seem easy compared to the amount of data that has to be moved around," he told The Register.

"In the biological space and sciences in general, it's not well appreciated just how big the data actually is," he added. "The internet's big, but in the grand scheme of things, the text that people are training on is actually pretty small, versus the petabytes of data that we have of these images."

Recursion's first cluster was a system called BioHive Zero which it cobbled together from GTX 1080 graphics cards originally intended for gaming systems. It used Ethernet to connect GPUs and ran into the limits of conventional networking.

So, for its second cluster, an A100-based machine with around 300 accelerators, Recursion went with Nvidia's Quantum InfiniBand switches and ConnectX NICs.

As Mabey explains it, buying its compute and interconnects from the same vendor vastly simplified optimization and troubleshooting. When Recursion reached out to Nvidia for help tuning the system, they were able to consider it as a single system, rather than discrete compute and networking resources. This level of integration had only previously been offered by boutique supercomputer builders like Cray or Atos (Eviden/Bull/whatever it gets renamed next).

A wider Spectrum

Nvidia hasn't always listed networking revenue as a single line item in its financials. However, based on CFO commentary and a little back of the napkin math we can fill in the blanks.

Nvidia's networking revenues have grown steadily since the Mellanox acquisition. At two moments, its revenue shot up suddenly.

The first came in the 2023 calendar year (FY24) after OpenAI kicked off the AI gold rush with the launch of ChatGPT.

The second spread across 2024 and 2025, when Nvidia started selling Blackwell-generation GPUs and then started to ship them in large quantities.

"I see two major inflection points, both coinciding with the Blackwell platform ramp: the introduction of NVLink switches and the accelerating adoption of Spectrum-X Ethernet switches for AI back-end networks, driven by growing deployments of Nvidia GPU clusters and increased attach rates of Nvidia switches, NICs, optics, and cables," Boujelbene said.

It was around this time that users started deploy to Ethernet switches in large volume across Nvidia clusters. Elon Musk's Colossus supercomputer was one of the earliest examples.

As we reported at the time, the first half of the 200,000-chip H100 and H200-based cluster came online in 2024 and, at the time, was among the largest ever clusters deployed using Nvidia's Spectrum-X Ethernet switches.

Meta has also made extensive use of Nvidia's Spectrum switches in its own training clusters, including at its million-GPU Prometheus site in New Albany, Ohio.

There are a couple reasons Nvidia's Spectrum-X offering is doing so well.

First, through a combination of Spectrum Ethernet switches, superNICs, and some clever software optimizations, Nvidia was able to replicate many of the qualities that made InfiniBand so attractive while also benefiting from significantly higher radix and interoperability.

Ethernet switches, including Nvidia's own, have historically offered more ports. The higher the radix, the fewer switches are necessary to support a cluster at a given port speed.

This often gets misconstrued to mean that InfiniBand can't scale beyond clusters of a certain size, which isn't necessarily the case. But for clusters exceeding a certain number of GPUs, Ethernet is often more economical because it requires less equipment while achieving similar utilization.

That said, Nvidia's Spectrum-X isn't exactly unique. Broadcom, Cisco, AMD and others have managed to achieve utilization similar to InfiniBand through various investments in smartNICs and switches.

Nvidia's main differentiator, as Mabey and Boujelbene noted earlier, is the level of integration between the compute and networking.

The next frontier

Then Blackwell hit the market, enabling much greater scale and creating a surge in Nvidia's networking revenue.

Prior to Blackwell, Nvidia's NVLink and NVSwitch interconnect tech had largely been limited to single systems with up to eight GPUs. With the launch of the GB200 NVL72, these NVSwitches were broken out into dedicated appliances. (And yes, we remember GH200 NVL256 and NVL32, but from what we can tell these were never shipped in any meaningful volume, if at all.)

For every NVL72 rack system sold, Nvidia also sold nine NVSwitch trays, which enabled the system's six dozen GPUs to function as a single enormous GPU. This effectively guaranteed steady networking revenue growth: even if customers didn't buy Nvidia's scale-out switches, if they wanted NVL72 racks, they were automatically buying NVSwitch.

"We estimate that NVSwitch now accounts for roughly one-third of Nvidia’s networking revenue, up from virtually zero less than two years ago," Boujelbene told El Reg. "This underscores how quickly NVLink switching has become a major growth engine for Nvidia’s networking business."

Nvidia is now moving to grow its networking business even further with a new licensing program.

Until recently, Nvidia's NVSwitch tech was limited to its own accelerators. However, with the introduction of NVLink Fusion early last year, Nvidia opened the interconnect tech up to third parties to license.

Many chip designers, including Marvell, Qualcomm, Arm, Fujitsu, MediaTek, and d-Matrix, have announced plans to develop CPUs and XPUs using NVLink Fusion. The adoption of the interconnect opens the door to additional NVSwitch sales, as the chips using NVLink Fusion still rely on them for chip-to-chip communications, and there's no other place to get them, as Nvidia has yet to license out NVSwitch.

"NVLink Fusion allows Nvidia to extend its networking footprint beyond its own GPUs into the rapidly growing custom accelerator market. I see it as a strategic move to capture a larger share of AI networking spending, even in clusters that do not use Nvidia GPUs," Boujelbene said.

Nvidia's networking ambitions don't stop there.

"Nvidia continues to pursue opportunities in emerging networking domains as network requirements evolve to keep pace with increasingly demanding AI workloads," Boujelbene added. "The latest example is scale-in networking, designed to address the new requirements of agentic AI, while innovations such as CPO will help overcome growing bandwidth, power, and scalability challenges." ®

Breakfast News: One Stock Outside the U.S. We Like

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The U.S. stock market is the largest in the world and home to some of the most valuable and iconic companies. It’s also where we spend most of our time at The Motley Fool. Yet plenty of outstanding businesses list only on their home exchanges and rarely show up on the radar of investors in the U.S. They’re well within reach, though, and can be bought through over-the-counter (OTC) tickers or a broker’s global trading desk.

For this edition of Breakfast News, we asked analysts from the different countries where TMF operates to each pick one stock that isn’t listed on a U.S. exchange and tell us why they like it. Here’s what they said.

Every cutting-edge chip — AI graphics processing unit (GPU), high bandwidth memory (HBM) stack, smartphone central processing unit (CPU) — starts with a photomask, made possible by ASML’s (NASDAQ:ASML) EUV technology. Almost nobody can verify that the mask is defect-free except Lasertec(OTC:LSRCY).

1 Unstoppable ETF I'm Buying and Holding for Decades -- Even if the Stock Market Crashes

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The past four years have been one of the best stretches ever for the Vanguard Morningstar Growth ETF (NYSEMKT: VUG). Since the beginning of 2023, the fund has returned just over 150%. Its heavy overweighting in technology stocks, currently 72% of the portfolio, and the tailwind from the artificial intelligence (AI) boom have combined to make a lot of money for shareholders.

The flip side is that if the stock market ever crashes, those factors that made it an outperformer could easily turn it into a deep underperformer.

But that's the price of admission for investing in growth stocks. And it's a big part of the reason why I'd buy and hold this fund for decades, even through a market crash. The potential for above-average long-term returns, despite the volatility, is worth owning.

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Leandro Cabrera's goal and Ángel Fortuño's saves gave Espanyol a 1-0 win at the Estadio de la Cerámica, with B-team coach Sergio García on the touchline. The victory came three days after Manolo González was sacked and before Eder Sarabia's official presentation.

Sergio García, the B-team coach, led the side from the touchline at the Estadio de la Cerámica. The game was the opening match of the LaLiga round.

The result came three days after a change at the top. Manolo González had led the first team in 107 official matches, Libertad Digital reported. He went into that cup tie with only one point from Espanyol’s previous 15 available in LaLiga.

Fortuño’s saves protected Cabrera’s goal against a dominant Villarreal

Villarreal controlled the ball for long spells. They completed 503 accurate passes to Espanyol’s 263. They also had 14 shots to Espanyol’s 5. Their expected goals figure was 0.92, against 0.26 for the visitors.

Espanyol thought they had taken the lead first. Roberto Fernández had a goal cancelled by VAR in the 50th minute. The review found he had held Pau Navarro before scoring. Fernández has six goals in LaLiga this season.

Cabrera then scored in the 60th minute. Bryan Zaragoza provided the assist. From there Espanyol defended their lead. Ángel Fortuño’s goalkeeping earned him the match’s top player rating of 8.31.

Villarreal had problems before half-time. Gerard Moreno went off with a muscle injury in the 17th minute and Nicolas Pépé replaced him. Foyth and Diatta had already been ruled out late. Iñigo Pérez’s side finished without a goal despite their territorial control.

The match was competitive without being dirty. Each team received three yellow cards and there were no red cards.

Sarabia takes charge with Deportivo next at home

Espanyol named Eder Sarabia head coach on Wednesday 28 October. He could not take charge at La Cerámica because he is serving a two-match suspension, Ara reported.

Sarabia’s new staff includes former centre-back Álvaro González. He played for Espanyol from 2014 to 2017 before moving to Olympique de Marseille.

Digital office communication affects stress, creativity, and perceived tone

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Office messages used to move on paper.

People used to type, copy, and file them by hand, but now they arrive in seconds via email, Slack, Microsoft Teams, text, and more. Trading these messages can feel like a conversation, but that feeling misleads people because the writer hears a tone of voice the reader often does not.

Nicholas Epley, a behavioral scientist at the University of Chicago, described the problem when email was a new, fast tool.

“But what’s different in this medium is…the ease with which we can fire things back and forth. It makes text-based communication seem more informal and more like face-to-face communication than it really is,” said Epley.

Researchers in psychology, management, and communication at universities in the United States have since tested the changes new tools bring to work.

Most of their studies are small. Together, they cover how well tone comes through on a screen, how stressed workers feel, and how teams come up with ideas when they aren’t in the same room.

Memos were built for filing

JoAnne Yates, a management professor emerita at MIT Sloan School of Management, studied how American companies built their first internal communication systems.

Her book Control Through Communication follows typewriters, carbon copies, and filing systems as they changed business writing in the late 1800s and early 1900s.

Cabinets that sorted papers by subject worked best for documents covering one subject each. The internal memo grew up alongside them, a one-topic document a company could copy, file, and find again.

Speed came much later. Yates worked with Wanda Orlikowski of MIT and Melissa Mazmanian of the University of California, Irvine, on a study of professionals who carried mobile email devices. At first, the devices gave those workers flexibility and peace of mind.

Mobile email also raised everyone’s expectations for how quickly someone should respond. The team wrote that professionals chose to work “anywhere, anytime,” but in practice they ended up working “everywhere, all the time.”

Tone gets lost in text

Epley and psychologist Justin Kruger tested how well tone travels in an experiment with 30 pairs of college students. Each student received a list of 20 short statements and gave each one a sarcastic or a serious tone.

One student in each pair read the statements into a tape recorder, and the other emailed them. Both groups expected their partners to read the tone right about 78% of the time.

Listeners got it right about 75% of the time. Email readers managed 56%, not much better than a coin flip.

The voice messages came close to what the senders hoped, and the emails fell well short. Still, readers and listeners alike guessed they judged the tone correctly 90% of the time.

Kruger and Epley blamed egocentrism, the difficulty people have seeing things from someone else’s point of view. A writer hears the intended tone, but the reader sees only words.

“E-mail is fine if you just want to communicate content, but not any emotional material,” said Epley.

Short replies now carry signals of their own.  In a Binghamton University study led by psychologist Celia Klin, 126 undergraduates rated one-word replies such as “Okay” and “Sure.” A period made those replies seem less sincere in a text message, but not in a handwritten note.

Email turned into a stress signal

Stephen Barley and Debra Meyerson of Stanford University and Stine Grodal of Boston University studied workers at high-tech companies.

Engineers, technical writers and marketers logged every phone call, email and face-to-face conversation over a series of workdays. The team reported in Organization Science that people who spent more time handling email felt more overloaded.

In later interviews, Grodal noted that participants spent about 90% of their discussion on email. Questions about phone calls and meetings drew little emotional response.

“You can actually see it on the screen; it has material properties. And so email becomes your to-do list of the day,” said Grodal.

Gloria Mark, an informatics professor at the University of California, Irvine, tested the opposite. Her team cut off email for 13 civilian employees at an Army research center outside Boston for five workdays. Heart rate monitors and computer software tracked the workers the whole time.

With email, the workers switched computer windows about 37 times an hour. Without it, they switched about 18 times, and Mark’s team used that switching to measure multitasking. Their heart rates also varied more during the email break, which the researchers read as a sign of lower stress.

Video narrows people’s focus

As more people started working from home, many meetings moved onto video. Melanie Brucks of Columbia Business School and Jonathan Levav of Stanford University tested what that does to brainstorming.

The researchers split 602 people into pairs and gave each pair five minutes to list creative uses for a Frisbee or bubble wrap. Some pairs sat together in a rooms, and others worked over video. The video pairs came up with fewer ideas, and fewer creative ones.

Eye tracking pointed to a reason. People on video spent more time looking at their partner and less time looking around the room. They also remembered fewer of the props the researchers had set out, such as a large houseplant.

The same pattern appeared among 1,490 engineers at company sites in five countries, although video didn’t hurt the next step: picking the best idea.

“When you’re visually narrowing yourself… you’re putting blinders on, basically,” said Brucks.

Video can also wear people out. In a Stanford survey of more than 10,000 people, about 13.8% of women and 5.5% of men said video calls left them very to extremely tired. The team found that the subjects seeing their own faces on screen for hours helped explain that gap.

Off-hours email raises anxiety

The pressure doesn’t end when the workday ends. William Becker of Virginia Tech, Liuba Belkin of Lehigh University and Samantha Conroy of Colorado State University surveyed full-time employees and their partners about checking email outside work hours.

Workers felt more anxious when employers expected them to monitor email at night, even if the workers spent little time actually working, and their partners reported worse health and less relationship satisfaction. After-hours email is a source of job stress that permeates the whole household.

“If the nature of a job requires email availability, such expectations should be stated formally as a part of job responsibilities,” said Becker.

Most of this research predates Slack and Teams becoming everyday tools. The tone experiment used email between college students, and the punctuation study used text messages, so these studies didn’t test workplace chat apps directly.

Grodal has said her team needs to return to workplaces and test changes such as letting workers skip after-hours replies. For now, those changes remain untested in the field.

The full study was published in the Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.

How to get involved

Read the Center for Plain Language’s free guides if you write messages to coworkers.  After the General Services Administration redirected the government’s plain language website in 2025, the nonprofit reposted the Federal Plain Language Guidelines

Explore the NIOSH Total Worker Health program if you help shape workplace policy. The federal program works to make jobs safe and to support workers’ health and well-being.

Follow the Society for Industrial and Organizational Psychology for research behind workplace practices. Its members study how people work, and the society shares that research with policymakers.

Try the National Day of Unplugging each March. This day calls for a 24-hour break from phones and computers that runs from sundown to sundown.

Meet the Brilliant AI Stock Obliterating Nvidia in 2026 With a Return of 44%

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia (NASDAQ: NVDA) supplies the world's best graphics processing units (GPUs) for data centers, which are the main chips used in artificial intelligence (AI) workloads. Since early 2023, the company has experienced more demand than it can possibly supply, which is why its market capitalization has ballooned from $360 billion to over $5.2 trillion as of this writing (Oct. 28).

But Nvidia is so large now that its best gains might be in the rearview mirror. In fact, its stock has returned just 16% so far in 2026. Many suppliers of other data center hardware components have performed much better this year, as the benefits of the AI infrastructure spending boom spread beyond the major chip makers.

Shares of Corning Inc (NYSE: GLW), for instance, have surged by 44% this year. The company has manufactured glass for some of the most innovative electronics since 1851, including Thomas Edison's original lightbulb, and every single generation of Apple's iPhone. Today, Corning is at the center of the AI boom thanks to its fiber-optic data center cables, and here's why its stock could deliver more upside over the long term.

Google's First Data Center Satellite Is Online and Writing Poetry

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Google's first experimental space-based data center is operational after launching earlier this month, successfully running its Gemma 4 AI model aboard a satellite and generating a haiku about being in orbit. TechCrunch reports: The satellite, which carries four Google Tensor Processing Units (TPUs) and generates up to 1 kW of electricity, is intended to pave the way for future networks of close-flying satellites performing large inference workloads in orbit. However, the power demands of the TPU mean it can only turn one on for about 20 minutes at a time, complicating the already tricky business of space-to-ground communications. After a commissioning process that tested out the satellite's subsystems over about three weeks, the spacecraft was ready to work. When engineers on the ground sent their first prompt this week, they asked the model pre-loaded onto the satellite, Google DeepMind's Gemma 4, to write a haiku about being in space. And thus: Blue marble below Data streams in silent grace Starlight feeds my core. The ongoing test campaign will examine how the chips perform under thermal stress and in the space radiation environment. [...] If the TPUs onboard this spacecraft prove capable, the next step will be launching two more satellites equipped with the advanced chips in 2027. Those spacecraft will support higher power generation and demonstrate inter-satellite optical links.

Read more of this story at Slashdot.
