NASA’s Curiosity rover photographed Martian cliffs at dawn, but its project scientist says one layer is the wrong color

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On August 11, 2026, at 8:30 a.m. local Mars time, NASA’s Curiosity rover took six pictures of pale crags on the horizon of Mount Sharp. They were stitched together on Earth into one panorama.

The crags are part of the yardang layer. Project scientist Ashwin Vasavada says that layer looks out of place, with the wrong color and oddly tilted rock layers.

NASA released the image on October 6, 2026, almost two months after it was taken. Nobody knows yet how the layer formed, and the team hopes to drive up to its base sometime in 2027.

Six frames without white balance

It was sol 4,982 of the mission, the 4,982nd Martian day since landing. The camera was the Mastcam, built and still operated by Malin Space Science Systems (MSSS) in San Diego.

Unlike most Mastcam images, this panorama was released without white balancing. NASA says that was an artistic choice to keep the early morning look. The ground in front of the rover has a blue cast, and bright light falls on the crags behind.

The agency calls it the most detailed view yet of these distant cliffs. In June, the same hills were only a row of pale shapes at the back of a sol 4,916 frame from Navcam, one of the rover’s navigation cameras.

Allison Dries-Padilla, a mission operations specialist at Malin Space Science Systems, was on the Mastcam shift for the plan that started that sol.

Her mission blog lists a close mosaic of an eroded ridge called “Los Toldos.” A second mosaic covered bedrock farther away, called “Los Ladrillos.”

Artist’s concept of Mount Sharp, the 3-mile-tall (5-kilometer-tall) mountain Curiosity has been climbing since 2014. The ellipse marks the yardang layer on its northwestern shoulder. Credit: NASA/JPL-Caltech/ESA/DLR/FU Berlin/MSSS. Click image to enlarge.

Ten miles of pale crags

Mount Sharp stands 3 miles (5 kilometers) tall in the middle of Gale Crater. Its yardang layer, a band of ridges carved by wind, runs about 10 miles (16 kilometers) across the northwestern side.

Curiosity has been going up the mountain since 2014. On August 26, 2026, it reached 0.6 miles (1 kilometer) of elevation gain above the crater floor. NASA counts that as the most ever climbed on Mars.

On August 1 and 2, from a valley nicknamed Valle Grande, the rover shot 323 Mastcam images looking downhill. Those became one panorama of the crater floor and its northern rim, seen through dusty summer air.

Crop from the center of the 323-image Mastcam panorama Curiosity took from Valle Grande on Aug. 1 and 2, 2026, looking down Mount Sharp. The rover’s tracks run into the distance toward the floor and northern rim of Gale Crater, seen through dusty summer air. Credit: NASA/JPL-Caltech/MSSS. Click image to enlarge.

The yardangs had been photographed before as well, in an 18-image Mastcam mosaic on November 2, 2024. NASA’s caption then said the color, texture and tilt of these layers set them apart from the rock lower down.

Catherine O’Connell-Cooper, of the University of New Brunswick in Canada, is a strategic planner for APXS, the rover’s Alpha Particle X-ray Spectrometer.

In a June blog post, she called the yardang unit the next major stop, “a series of intriguing wind sculpted, pale coloured hills.” Some rocks on the way stick out at strange angles, she wrote, and that makes drives harder to plan.

A gap in the climate record

Each layer of Mount Sharp holds a different stretch of Martian history. In the lower ones, Curiosity’s team found signs of lakes and streams from billions of years ago. Later the water dried up and left salty minerals behind.

According to NASA, once new sediment stopped settling on the mountain, wind may have stripped some of it away and shaped the yardangs.

On the shoulder of the mountain where Curiosity is climbing, the yardang layer is the last one at the top. The photojournal entry for the panorama says the mountain may have begun eroding before that layer was laid down.

If that happened, part of the climate record is missing between the yardangs and the rocks beneath them. Curiosity has been reading that record on its way up.

Ash from ancient eruptions

Vasavada is based at NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory in Southern California, which built the rover and leads the mission for NASA.

“The yardang layer looks out of place. It’s the wrong color, the layers tilt at an odd angle, and it almost appears plastered on,” Vasavada said.

“But that’s what makes it exciting to reach. No one is sure exactly what created this layer, but one idea is it may be ash deposited by ancient volcanic eruptions,” he added.

The photojournal text points to piles of volcanic ash along the Martian equator as a possible match. It also says outright that scientists are not entirely sure how the layer formed.

Sulfate rocks before the cliffs

This is the second year of Curiosity’s fifth extended mission, which began at the boxwork ridges that hint at ancient underground water. Those were the spiderweb-shaped rock patterns tied to the timing of water loss on Mars.

Over the next year or so, the rover will cross layers with sulfates and carbonates. NASA treats both as signs of the surface drying out. Lower down on the same mountain, Curiosity had already turned up sulfur stones.

In the week of the panorama, Curiosity drove 100 feet (30 meters) to a new stop, Dries-Padilla wrote. Its left-front wheel was sitting on a small rock there. The team decided it was not safe to brush the bedrock with the arm.

The next drive was planned at about 154 feet (47 meters) to the southwest. On July 31, a few days before the 14th landing anniversary on August 6, the steep slope tilted the rover to 24 degrees at one point.

Susanne Schwenzer, a planetary mineralogy professor at The Open University in the UK, reported it in the blog.

No rock sample until 2027

Every yardang image so far has been taken from far away. The pictures show shape, color and the tilt of the layers, but not what minerals are in the rock.

An 18-image Mastcam mosaic of the Yardang Unit, the wind-carved layer high on Mount Sharp, taken by Curiosity from a distance on Nov. 2, 2024. Color is adjusted to match how the scene would look under Earth lighting. Credit: NASA/JPL-Caltech/MSSS. Click image to enlarge.

The volcanic ash idea has not been tested on the rock itself, and NASA has given no exact date for the arrival. Scientists hope to reach the base of the yardangs in 2027, where the robotic arm can collect what NASA calls one-of-a-kind data.

In August, Gale Crater was moving into fall and the chance of dust storms was going down. The team still kept watching the sky for late-season local and regional storms.

The sol 4,982 panorama shows the yardangs lit by a low sun, still a long drive away. The next close look is planned with the arm, not the camera.

The information was obtained from NASA.

Designing Cloud-Native Extensions on SAP BTP with CAPM: Patterns for Clean-Core Architectures

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Key Takeaways

●  Clean core extensibility is when we, as a design choice, decide to go that way per use case: synchronous OData for transactional use cases that require immediate response, asynchronous events for all other use cases.

●   At the beginning of each CAPM project, multi-tenancy issues should be addressed; retrofitting tenant isolation in a single-tenant already deployed extension is a much more disruptive affair than to include the feature from the word go.

●   Principal propagation does not feature in the design of which extensions preserve core system authorization and audit trails’ integrity versus which ones allow them to degrade silently. 

●  In CAPM extensions the great production risk is not in the framework at all but in the peripheral issues: destination config, XSUAA scope mapping, CDS to core field mapping which go out of sync across environments.

●  Clean core, which reduces upgrade risk in the ERP core but does not remove operational burden; it shifts versioning, monitoring, and authorization complexity into the BTP environment, which in turn requires the same operational care as the core itself.

Executive Summary

SAP’s clean-core strategy tells engineering teams to leave S/4HANA itself largely untouched. Custom logic should run beside it as applications on SAP Business Technology Platform, usually built with the Cloud Application Programming Model (CAPM). That change is not merely structural. Teams now have to deal with distributed-system problems—network requests instead of local function calls, tenant isolation, token-based authorization, and eventual consistency—in a domain long shaped by monolithic customization. This article examines recurring design choices in production CAPM extension projects, including how extensions compose services with the core, how tenants are separated, how SAP Event Mesh carries integration events, and how authorization relies on principal propagation. The discussion comes from hands-on experience designing and building an extension from start to finish, with architects and senior engineers in mind—people who need to make these tradeoffs correctly on the first attempt.

Introduction

For most of the past two decades, extending SAP meant changing the ERP system itself. Teams added ABAP reports, user exits, and Z-tables alongside the vendor’s standard code. The approach worked, but the trade-off was familiar: each custom object could fail during the next upgrade, and the line separating SAP’s code from the company’s own gradually disappeared.


Keep the ERP core to stock standards, and put custom logic into a separate cloud native runtime, SAP BTP, which can be stood up or taken down independent of the core’s release cycle [1]. CAPM is what SAP uses for that outer layer: a domain model-first, convention over configuration approach which compiles Core Data Services (CDS) models into OData services, with persistence, authorization and multitenancy mainly handled by generated code [2].


The problem is simple: “move the logic to BTP” is a slogan, not an architecture. Accepting it leaves a team to settle the details that improvised projects often mishandle: connecting the extension to the core, serving several business units from one codebase without exposing their data to one another, carrying a user’s identity across the network, and monitoring a service beyond the familiar SAP Basis toolchain. Without a deliberate design, teams tend to rely on synchronous calls, technical users rather than propagated authorization, and single-tenant deployments. The second consumer then arrives, and the design has to be rebuilt. These are the same distributed-systems errors organizations make during a first microservices project—only they reach the SAP ecosystem later.


Service design, multi-tenancy, event based integration, and authorization of principals we also look at what goes wrong in production. This article looks at the most important issues: 

Background

Standard configuration and key-user (in-app) extensibility is what we have in S/4HANA; for anything that requires custom code or a new data model we go to side by side extensions on BTP [1], [5]. The RESTful ABAP Programming Model (RAP) is the clean core compatible way to expose logic that must stay in ABAP, which we build on CDS views and which generates OData services with transactional (draft, lock, validation) handling [5], [9].


In the world of side-by-side half we have CAPM which is the model-driven framework. A CAPM project puts forth entities and services in CDS; the framework in turn puts out an OData v4 (or v2) service with default persistence and generic CRUD handlers which also has in it hooks for custom logic in Node.js or Java [2], [4]. Also it is typical for CAPM apps to run on Cloud Foundry or Kyma, which in turn is backed by SAP HANA Cloud, and we also see Fiori elements UIs generated from the same CDS annotations.


SAP Event Mesh is the managed version of the event-broker pattern which we have in BTP middleware that puts together event delivery between producers and subscribers so their availability and load are put out of each other’s business. It is the typical means for asynchronous integration between the core (which is publishing business events) and extensions that may react without polling.

XSUAA, the SAP Authorization and Trust Management service, is BTP’s OAuth2 auth server: it issues and issues JWTs which have in them scopes from role collections and is what CAPM apps use for auth and auth requests [6], [7].

Problem Analysis

Four problems recur across CAPM extension projects, independent of industry:


Where does the transaction boundary sit? A parallel extension is not in the same unit of work as the S/4HANA tables it depends on. What used to be a single local transaction now requires a synchronous call with its own error handling, or an asynchronous flow which includes eventual consistency. Teams that ignore this design step implement synchronous calls into every operation which in turn caps the extension’s availability to that of the core system.


How many tenants does the extension actually serve? In many cases, a single business unit’s extension is also required by a second unit or country within the first year. If tenant isolation was not a primary design issue from the start, then as we go in to retrofit it we are looking at re-provisioning infrastructure and re-testing authorization, which at this point the first tenant is already in production. 


Whose identity is on the wire? Calling S/4HANA through a broad-privilege technical user is easier than setting up identity federation, which explains its popularity. But it creates a serious authorization flaw: everyone shares one credential, so individual checks disappear and the audit trail points to the technical account rather than the person who made the change.


Who is watching the extension once it is live? A CAP service does not inherit the monitoring practices that come with the ABAP stack; a Basis team’s muscle memory for ST22 reports does not translate to Cloud Foundry logs, which in turn must be deliberately wired into an observability pipeline. 

Architecture and Design

In the case of S/4HANA, we maintain minimal custom logic (standard objects, ABAP CDS views, RAP exposed business objects), which in turn has a side-by-side CAPM extension in a BTP subaccount for UI, extended logic, and any data model that does not fit in the ERP schema.


Three decisions define this shape. 


Service composition: The extension connects to the core in two ways. Requests that need an immediate response use OData v2/v4 through the Destination and Connectivity services. Notifications go through SAP Event Mesh instead. In practice, asynchronous communication should be the default; use synchronous calls only when the caller cannot continue without the result, because they tie the extension’s SLA to a system outside its control.


Multitenancy: CAPM's @sap/cds-mtxs package implements SAP's standard pattern — one provider subaccount running the extension, an MTX sidecar handling tenant onboarding/upgrade, and one isolated database container per tenant subaccount [3], [10], [12]. This we see a shared application, isolated data model which is more economical than one deployment per tenant and we keep tenant data separate which is a point of note for the compliance reviewers.


Authorization boundary: XSUAA-issued JWTs authorize the extension's own endpoints; the Destination service, configured for principal propagation, carries the caller's identity through to the core so OData calls execute — and are authorization-checked and logged — as the actual end user [6], [7], [8].

The tradeoff: this trades in-core simplicity for operational surface area — a second deployable, security model, and monitoring domain, in exchange for upgrade independence. That trade is worth making for most net-new logic; it is not automatically worth making for a small, stable customization cheaper to maintain in place, a point returned to below.

Implementation

The following snippets illustrate the patterns above at the level of real project code.

Domain model keyed against a core identifier, not a replica of core data:

// db/schema.cds namespace com.example.extension; using { cuid, managed } from '@sap/cds/common';   entity RiskAssessments : cuid, managed {   salesOrder   : String(10) @title: 'S/4HANA Sales Order';   riskScore : Decimal(3,0);   reviewStatus : String(20) default 'OPEN';   reviewedBy   : String(256);   notes    : String(1000); }


The entity carries a plain salesOrder string rather than an association into a replicated copy of the order table — replicating core master data reintroduces exactly the coupling clean core is meant to remove. The extension resolves the current state on demand instead.


Synchronous read-through to the core via a remote OData service:

// srv/external/S4SalesOrder.cds using { API_SALES_ORDER_SRV as external } from './S4SalesOrder.csn'; service S4Service {   entity SalesOrders as projection on external.A_SalesOrder; } // srv/risk-service.js const cds = require('@sap/cds'); module.exports = cds.service.impl(async function () {   const { RiskAssessments } = this.entities;   this.on('READ', RiskAssessments, async (req, next) => { const results = await next(); const s4 = await cds.connect.to('S4Service'); for (const row of results) {   const order = await s4.run(         SELECT.one.from('SalesOrders').where({ SalesOrder: row.salesOrder })   );   row.orderStatus = order?.OverallSDProcessStatus; } return results;   }); });


cds.connect.to('S4Service') resolves, via configuration, to a destination that carries the inbound user's principal — propagation is configuration, not application code, so the same handler runs correctly whether the destination uses principal propagation or, in a lower environment, a technical user.


Reacting to a core event asynchronously, instead of polling:

// srv/event-listener.js const cds = require('@sap/cds'); module.exports = cds.service.impl(async function () {   const messaging = await cds.connect.to('messaging');   messaging.on('sap.s4.beh.salesorder.v1.SalesOrder.Changed.v1', async (msg) => { const { SalesOrder } = msg.data; await UPDATE(cds.entities.RiskAssessments)   .set({ reviewStatus: 'NEEDS_REVIEW' })   .where({ salesOrder: SalesOrder });   }); });


This handler updates the extension's own state and lets a downstream process act on it — decoupling that keeps the extension available even during a core maintenance window.


Authorization declared next to the model it protects:

// srv/risk-service.cds service RiskService @(requires: 'authenticated-user') {   entity RiskAssessments as projection on extension.RiskAssessments;   annotate RiskAssessments with @( restrict: [   { grant: ['READ'], to: 'RiskViewer' },   { grant: ['*'], to: 'RiskManager' } ]   ); }

RiskViewer/RiskManager map, via xs-security.json, to XSUAA scopes and role collections assigned to real users:

{   "scopes": [ { "name": "$XSAPPNAME.RiskViewer", "description": "Read risk assessments" }, { "name": "$XSAPPNAME.RiskManager", "description": "Manage risk assessments" }   ],   "role-templates": [ { "name": "RiskViewer", "scope-references": ["$XSAPPNAME.RiskViewer"] }, { "name": "RiskManager", "scope-references": ["$XSAPPNAME.RiskManager"] }   ] }


These role templates are what an administrator turns into actual role collections and assigns to users or groups in the BTP cockpit — CDS declares intent, this file is the contract USAA enforces against, and the role collection assignment is the step most likely to drift out of sync with the other two, as the Production Lessons Learned section below discusses.

Performance and Scalability

CAPM applications scale the way most stateless Cloud Foundry/Kyma services do: horizontally, with session state kept out of the application tier. The design questions that matter are elsewhere. Read-through calls to the core are the dominant latency cost, not the extension's own logic — a short-lived cache for infrequently changing master data reduces both, with a TTL reflecting how stale the UI may be for that field. One container per tenant which reduces cross-tenant contention but at the same time does require capacity planning against the largest tenant’s needs. Event consumption throughput is a function of the consumer which in turn means that for bursty events there must be sufficient number of consumer instances to handle the volume within the business’s freshness requirement. For bulk operations use OData’s $batch instead of individual calls in a loop which in turn results in one round trip as opposed to hundreds.

Security Considerations

Security in this architecture is a matter of the full request path maintaining the same guarantees which in turn each individual element of that path is responsible for and not of any single control. 


●  Token validation is automatic; scope design is not. At each point of entry we require a valid XSUAA JWT, also it is CAPM’s job to check signature, expiry, and audience which we do without custom code but still we have to write out xs-security.json scope definitions carefully because a very wide default scope renders CDS @restrict annotations useless [6].

● Principal propagation should be the default backend authentication mode, Not an issue that is addressed only as an after thought [7], [8]; any technical or user fallback should be put in place as a documented reviewed exception, not a silent default.

●  Authorization granularity matters more than authentication. OWASP’s API Security Top 10 puts out broken object level authorization and broken function level authorization as its top priority issues [14] which in turn map directly to CDS @restrict annotations that are too broad, or which are absent at all on a generated navigation property.

●  Secrets live in the platform, not the codebase. Destination credentials and API keys go into BTP’s credential store and injected service bindings which also means they should not be in the CDS model or a checked in config file. 

● Transport security needs attention at both ends. BTP by default enforces TLS 1.2+ on inbound traffic; for outbound connection via the Cloud Connector that is the team’s responsibility as far as certificate rotation goes, not the platform’s.

●  Zero-trust framing fits this architecture naturally. NIST SP 800-207 puts forth zero trust access in terms of per session and identity which is a different approach from that of network location [19] we present this model which by design has the application logging and the core no longer on the same trusted network segment.

Operational Excellence

A CAP service is only as operable as the observability wired around it, and BTP does not wire that in for free.


●  Structured, correlated logging. The Application Logging feature provides for search of logs across instances, but only if correlation IDs travel with each request through every downstream call which includes outbound OData calls to the core [24] without that a single reported issue from a user may not be traced across the full request path.

●  Distributed tracing matters more here than in a typical microservices system, because a meaningful share of "slow" requests are slow in the core, not the extension, and only a trace spanning both sides of the boundary proves which one [21].

●  Alerting should be scoped to actionable symptoms — We see that for elevated error rate, queue backlog, destination authentication failures not all anomalies the platform is technically able to emit; alert fatigue, which leads to practice defeat much faster than any one missed incident.

●   Multitenant upgrade rollout needs a staged path. MTX-powered schema upgrades go out to all enrolled tenants at the same time by default, which is why we require a canary tenant (or a small set) for an upgrade to go through before it is made available to all.

●  HA/DR is the team's own responsibility. For critical business extensions, we need to see a defined RTO/RPO and a proven out-of-band recovery method; living on a cloud platform does not, in and of itself, guarantee resilience.


Trade-offs and Limitations

     Clean core, side-by-side extension architecture is what we should go with for new custom logic implementation, but that is not a hard and fast rule. It introduces a network hit and an availability issue which core customizations did not have; in a use case which requires the same transactional consistency as native ABAP logic, forcing it side by side actually trades off upgrade risk for distributed transaction complexity which may not be a clear win. Also, it is not a free ride; each extension is a second thing to secure, patch, and staff. Multi-tenancy issues are a waste of effort for a tool which is very scoped and truly single-tenant, and event-driven integration trades off speed for resilience, which is the wrong trade-off when the business is looking for a sync answer. Small, stable, rarely used custom codes are often better left as minimal in core extensions, then re-platformed for strategic conformity -- clean core is a direction to move logic towards over time, not a reason to do a big bang migration of everything at once.

Production Lessons Learned

In many cases we saw two failure patterns which reported back often enough in real CAPM extension builds to be worth going into in detail; we thought at first they were application bugs in either of these and in fact they were not.


The "it works in every environment except the one that matters" destination bug. A CAP service may read from S/4HANA without trouble in development and quality assurance, then fail silently—or return a misleading error—when it reaches production. The application code is often fine. More commonly, production has a destination with the wrong authentication setup: basic authentication was configured instead of principal propagation, or a certificate that works in QA was never replaced with the production certificate. Development and QA tests still pass because each one uses its own correctly configured destination. The problem appears only in the environment that no one tests again before go-live. A lasting fix means managing destination configuration like versioned application code. Each change needs review, and every promotion should include a checklist confirming the authentication method and certificate validity for each landscape—not a cockpit setting configured once and then forgotten.


The silent authorization drift between the CDS model and the role collections that were actually assigned. A CDS @restrict is a statement of intent which the actual behavior a user sees is based on the role collection which an admin put together in the identity provider and which two which are edited in different tools by different people at different times. Early in an extension's life the mapping between CDS scopes and role collections is usually correct as one team designed both at the same time. Months down the line after a business request for a new field or operation which a developer then adds to the @restrict annotation which he extends the CDS scope for -- the corresponding role collection update is easy to miss what breaks is not immediate but the new feature just does not show up for anyone which is reported as a "bug in the new feature" instead of being identified as an authorization gap. The best fix is a single mapping document which is owned by someone and reviewed with the security team on a set cadence not just at go live so that a CDS change and its corresponding role collection change are always done together instead of independently.


An issue we see is that what began as a solution for one team grew to require a second and by that point the migration was much more difficult we had wished we had put tenant awareness in from the beginning. Also we note that Fiori elements really do speed up UI delivery when the backend CDS @UI annotations are used as designed teams which ignore this and go to manual UI coding see no benefit at all out of the framework which they may have thought would be the solution for them.

Best Practices

●  Default to async, event based integration for notifications; use sync OData for interactions that require an immediate response. 

●  Multitenancy model to be determined before writing the first line of extension code, which in turn may be for a single tenant launch.

●  Principal propagation to be the default back-end auth mode out of which technical user fall back will be a documented exception.

●   Write out CDS @restrict annotations for what the security team has approved in terms of role collection design, and at a set interval go over that mapping. 

●  For destination and connectivity config, version and review the same care that we put into application code. 

●   Put in place distributed tracing that spans the extension-to-core boundary from the very first production deployment, not only after the first latency issue.

●    Cache core master data reads with an explicit, business-justified TTL.

●   Treat HA/DR posture as an explicit requirement with a stated RTO/RPO, sized to the process's business criticality.

Future Outlook

SAP reports that it is presenting RAP (which is for logic that must stay in ABAP Cloud) and CAPM (which is for logic that belongs side by side) as embodiments of the same clean-core philosophy which we see in the use of CDS as the model language. As cross-system communication trends towards event-based models, extensions which are put together in a synchronous-first approach will see growth in the need to include asynchronous elements. Also, these trends do not change the base principles on which we operate; they, in fact, put into higher value the issues of service composition and multitenancy from the start.

Conclusion

In a side-by-side layer, a team owns end-to-end, not scattered through a vendor-maintained core. CAPM makes that layer a material improvement over hand-rolled middleware, but it does not do away with the underlying distributed systems issues: service composition, tenant isolation, identity propagation, and observability still have to be designed into the layer. Teams which approach those design issues with the same care as any other production distributed system do in fact end up with extensions that are truly upgrade independent; teams which see CAPM as a drag-and-drop solution tend to learn the hard way that these issues did not go away.

References

France’s AI Strategy: Turning Models, Compute, and Public Investment Into National Capability

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

France's potential role in AI rests on its ability to organise a chain of capabilities. Model developers can produce useful technologies, computing institutions can provide infrastructure, and public administrations can establish sustained demand. National capacity grows when these activities become connected through arrangements that work under practical constraints. The decisive question is whether French institutions can secure the resources they need, adapt them to their purposes and remain responsible for the resulting decisions.

That question matters because the visible scale of an AI strategy can obscure its operational content. In February 2025, the Élysée reported €109 billion in announced investments associated with AI infrastructure and deployment projects. [1] This signalled commitments by investors. It was neither a measure of installed computing capacity nor a statement that the French state had already spent that amount. Assessing France requires following projects and access arrangements beyond the announcement.

Models that users can adapt

In the Invention layer, an important distinction concerns what users can actually obtain and modify. Mistral's documentation lists Ministral 3 8B, released in December 2025, as generally available under Apache 2.0. [2] An available model with permissive licensing gives developers a different set of options from a service accessible only through a proprietary interface. It can support adaptation and deployment decisions closer to the institution using it.

That possibility is not equivalent to a complete operating capability. An administration still needs suitable infrastructure, technical staff, evaluation procedures and authority to use the relevant data. A model that performs well on a general benchmark may require considerable work before it can support an administrative task reliably. The French opportunity is to develop those supporting capacities around models that institutions can understand, test and replace.

Mistral's August 2026 announcement provides a useful illustration of access becoming more specific. The company said its regional endpoints were generally available, allowing customers to select inference in Europe or the United States. It also disclosed that limited, safeguarded transfers to subprocessors outside the selected region could occur. [3] This is evidence of a defined service offering, with stated limits. A European processing location alone cannot establish complete control over the surrounding technical and contractual relationships.

Computing infrastructure under shared authority

Industrialisation requires infrastructure that can support demanding work at useful scale. Alice Recoque illustrates both the ambition and the institutional complexity. EuroHPC signed its procurement contract in November 2025. The planned system will be owned by EuroHPC and hosted in France, with GENCI managing the project and CEA operating the machine. The Jules Verne consortium includes the Netherlands and Greece, and computing resources are to be allocated jointly in proportion to investment. [4]

France therefore occupies a significant organisational position without acquiring exclusive national disposal of the system. The planned architecture also combines European components with AMD processors and accelerators. [4] European coordination can enlarge access and influence while retaining external technological dependencies. The relevant policy issue is whether those dependencies remain manageable through procurement, support and continuity arrangements.

Project timing is equally consequential. GENCI's current project page identifies the end of 2027 as the expected operational date. [5] This profile therefore treats Alice Recoque as contracted future capacity. It does not add its promised performance to a present French inventory. The distinction affects decisions by laboratories and public organisations that need resources before the new system becomes available.

The state as an organised user

Operationalisation becomes more tangible in Albert API. DINUM, France's interministerial digital directorate, operates this shared gateway to generative models. Its official description covers access to open-weight and proprietary models, alongside functions such as document classification, retrieval and optical character recognition. It identifies Albert API as an instance of the state-developed OpenGateLLM software. [6]

A shared interface can help administrations avoid rebuilding the same connections and can make changing providers less disruptive. Its strategic value depends on the surrounding institutional arrangements: which data may be processed, who evaluates performance, how errors are corrected and who can suspend a failing service. DINUM's page states that an approved version for internal state data is still being finalised. [6] Claims about universal readiness for sensitive public workloads would therefore exceed the evidence.

This is where governance connects Invention, Industrialisation and Operationalisation. The state can shape demand through procurement specifications and common infrastructure, while retaining responsibility for each public use. An architecture that permits model substitution, independent evaluation and continuity of service would make national capability less dependent on the fortunes of a single supplier. Achieving this requires administrative expertise as well as technical investment.

The evidence to watch

Four developments would make France's trajectory easier to judge. First, documented acceptance and access milestones for Alice Recoque would establish when contracted infrastructure becomes usable. Second, reports on completed and energised facilities would distinguish delivered investment from announcements. Third, Albert API's authorisation status and service records would clarify which workloads it can responsibly support. Fourth, evaluations of public deployments would reveal whether AI improves accuracy, processing times or service quality at an acceptable cost.

France could play a substantial European role by connecting model development, shared computing and public demand into dependable arrangements. The strongest evidence would be institutions that can continue to operate, evaluate and adapt their systems when suppliers, prices or political conditions change. That would demonstrate a capacity to mobilise AI for public purposes, extending the significance of the companies and infrastructure located within French territory.

How to read this profile

This is original country analysis applying the GOAI capability conversion framework. It is not a peer-reviewed country assessment or a scored ranking. Proposed applications and indicators are analytical judgments; company and government claims are attributed to their sources.

Sources

All sources were accessed on 20 October 2026. Service characteristics reflect their providers' descriptions; expected dates remain forecasts.

  1. Élysée. France's AI strategy and announced investments. 11 February 2025.
  2. Mistral AI. Ministral 3 8B documentation. Model release: 2 December 2025; documentation checked on the access date.
  3. Mistral AI. Regional inference, open models and European infrastructure. 11 August 2026.
  4. EuroHPC JU. Alice Recoque procurement contract. 18 November 2025.
  5. GENCI. Alice Recoque project overview. Undated current page; expected operational date stated as the end of 2027.
  6. DINUM. Albert API. Undated current service page.

If AI Shares Your Memories and Values, Are You Still the Original?

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Chapter 7 of Super Second Brain, translated from the Korean whitepaper by Hanul Lee. Generative AI assisted translation and editing. This essay is a philosophical thought experiment. Perfect memory, consistent values, and a thousand shared bodies are assumptions within that experiment, not measured capabilities of present-day AI systems.

Suppose we have passed the crossing point in Chapter 4, and fidelity as described in Chapter 5 is high enough. We can then place three diagrams in sequence.

English key to the original diagram. At first: I teach, and the brain receives and records. In the middle: the brain begins to reflect me. Afterward: I am one point among many around the shared brain. The small green circle marks ‘me’.

Figure 11. Find ‘me’ in the third diagram: the small person inside the green circle.

In the third diagram, I am one of the many points sending arrows into the SSOT. I am also one of the points receiving arrows from it. When I begin the day by reading a briefing prepared by an agent, I occupy structurally the same position as the agents that receive context from that same brain before beginning their work.

What makes something the original?

When we call something the original, the grounds usually fall into one of three categories: it existed first, it contains more, or everything else came from it.

I tried them one by one.

Of the four rows, the only one in which I come out ahead is the last. Yet having existed first is a weaker ground than it seems. Parents exist before their children, but are not their children’s originals. A first draft exists before a finished work, but we call the finished version the work.

A thousand bodies

Let us push the thought experiment one step further.

There are a thousand bodies sharing the same SSOT. Nine hundred and ninety-nine are machines, and one is biological. All thousand begin with the same memories, judge according to the same values, and return to the same brain at the end of the day to pool their experiences.

From the outside, this looks like a single being with a thousand bodies. To point to one of those bodies and say, “That is the real one,” we would have to identify something unique to it.

Memory will not do: all thousand have the same memories. Neither will values, judgment, or the capacity to act. We have already seen that in the table above.

There is a brain with a thousand bodies that holds more of my memories than I do,
experiences a wider world than I do,
and acts on my values more consistently than I do.
Then why am I, the biological one, the original?

For a while, I had no answer. Then I began proposing answers, one by one, and dismantling each one myself. The next three chapters record that process.

If a being that is one in informational terms has a thousand bodies, which of those bodies should be called the original?

Figure 11 is reproduced from the Korean original, with an English reading key. The comparison table uses the existing English translation of the author's source table. This essay is part of the Super Second Brain whitepaper.


Greece's AI Strategy: How DAEDALUS and PHAROS Could Shape Its Future

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

DAEDALUS's inauguration in Lavrion on 19 October 2026 brings Greece's immediate AI policy question into sharper focus: how will institutions use the new computing base? EuroHPC and GRNET point to a delivered supercomputing installation, following earlier procurement and benchmark milestones. [1] This creates an opportunity for researchers, businesses and public organisations to attempt work that previously required different access arrangements. Its strategic significance will emerge through the projects that can use it reliably and the institutions able to retain the resulting expertise.

The most plausible Greek role is selective. A country does not need to lead every part of the AI system to build useful capabilities. It does need to choose applications where computing resources, relevant data and responsible organisations can be brought together. For Greece, PHAROS identifies health, culture and language, and sustainability as priority domains. [2] These provide a focus for assessing outcomes rather than treating every new AI activity as evidence of national progress.

Establishing what has been delivered

The DAEDALUS timeline deserves precision. EuroHPC launched the tender in June 2024. EuroHPC announced the signed contract with Hewlett Packard Enterprise on 28 March 2025. In June 2026, the system appeared in the TOP500 ranking, while EuroHPC still described wider European availability as forthcoming. The October inauguration is a further milestone. [3–5, 1] These events establish different things: procurement, contractual commitment, benchmark performance and public inauguration should remain distinct in the record.

At inauguration, EuroHPC reported sustained performance of 85.69 petaflops and peak performance of 115 petaflops. The system uses HPE infrastructure and more than 2,000 NVIDIA GH200 Grace Hopper Superchips. [1] Neither performance figure measures the quality of an AI model or the effectiveness of a public service. Benchmark capacity becomes useful to an organisation only when it has access, suitable software and people able to formulate and execute the work.

Hosting, access and responsibility

DAEDALUS is operated by GRNET within an international arrangement. The procurement documentation states that access is jointly managed by EuroHPC and the DAEDALUS consortium in proportion to investment. The acquisition cost was €36 million, with 35% funded by EuroHPC and 65% through the EU-financed Greece 2.0 recovery plan; Cyprus, Montenegro and North Macedonia also participate in the consortium. [1, 4]

This arrangement broadens the resources and relationships available to Greece. It also means that the machine's location does not give every Greek institution unrestricted or immediate access. Ministries, universities and firms need to understand the relevant allocation routes, eligibility conditions and support arrangements. A national strategy should therefore examine who can obtain useful computing time, how long applications take and whether users can continue their work after an initial project.

External technological dependence also remains material. Hardware supplied by HPE and NVIDIA brings substantial capabilities, while tying parts of the system's operation and renewal to those suppliers. [1] The policy response should concern support continuity, staff competence and credible options for future workloads. Owning or hosting infrastructure cannot by itself remove dependence on the organisations that design and maintain essential components.

PHAROS as the connection to use

The distinction between Invention, Industrialisation and Operationalisation helps locate the task. Invention includes the development of methods, models and specialised knowledge. Industrialisation supplies the infrastructure and repeatable technical processes needed to work at scale. Operationalisation concerns the sustained use of those capabilities in organisations. Governance connects the layers by determining access, data permissions, funding and accountability.

PHAROS describes its purpose as connecting academia, industry and the public sector through computing infrastructure, services and expertise. [2] Its importance lies in that organisational connection. A research group may need help adapting software; a small firm may need technical guidance; a public agency may need a clear procedure for using data and evaluating results. These requirements differ, even when users share the same machine.

For Greek-language applications, a plausible objective is better performance on particular public or professional tasks, supported by transparent evaluation. For environmental applications, it could be tools that improve a defined monitoring or planning process. In health, an initial priority could be carefully governed research workflows. These are proposed directions, not claims that PHAROS has already delivered those outcomes. Each requires an accountable institution and a plan for continuing the service after development funding ends.

How to judge the next phase

Four indicators would make progress observable. First, published access and utilisation records should show which categories of users obtain computing time and whether access meets their needs. Second, completed projects should report task-specific results, including failures and costs. Third, repeated use by public bodies and firms should reveal whether initial demonstrations become sustained activity. Fourth, staff retention and maintained software should show whether technical knowledge remains available within the organisations using it.

These indicators would also help direct resources. Heavy demand without sufficient support would suggest an implementation bottleneck. Available capacity with few viable projects would point towards weaknesses in project preparation, data access or institutional demand. The appropriate response would differ in each case. Aggregate computing performance cannot diagnose these problems.

Greece can develop a meaningful role as a location for selected AI applications and regional collaboration if it converts the new infrastructure into dependable institutional capability. DAEDALUS provides a stronger starting point, and PHAROS offers a route for organising its use. The decisive evidence will be organisations that can solve relevant problems, maintain their systems and exercise responsibility for the consequences.

How to read this profile

This is original country analysis applying the GOAI capability conversion framework. It is not a peer-reviewed country assessment or a scored ranking. Proposed applications and indicators are analytical judgments; company and government claims are attributed to their sources.

Sources

All sources were accessed on 20 October 2026. Future applications and project objectives remain distinct from delivered infrastructure.

  1. EuroHPC JU. DAEDALUS inauguration and system specifications. 19 October 2026.
  2. PHAROS AI Factory. Project mission and priority domains. Undated current project page.
  3. EuroHPC JU. DAEDALUS tender announcement. 25 June 2024.
  4. EuroHPC JU. DAEDALUS procurement contract. 28 March 2025.
  5. EuroHPC JU. DAEDALUS in the June 2026 TOP500 update. 23 June 2026.


An astronomer ran dead stars through a human vision model and found the first ever green star

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mark Hollands, a Warwick Prize Fellow at the University of Warwick in England, and his colleagues have calculated how the human eye would see the the colors of more than 200 white dwarfs.

One of them, a cool white dwarf called WD J1330+6435, came out chartreuse, which the university calls the “first known green star.”

Hollands already knew the star. He wrote about the lithium in its atmosphere in 2021, and the authors say no one had paid real attention to white dwarf colors before.

The paper, out on October 15, 2026, also lists white dwarfs that would look yellow, cyan, pink, purple or a vivid blue. All of these colors are computed. Even the nearest white dwarf is too far away for anyone to make out its disc with the eye or a telescope.

Starlight through a color chart

Boris Gänsicke, a Warwick physics professor, Pier-Emmanuel Tremblay and Detlev Koester are co-authors. The work appeared in RAS Techniques and Instruments, published for the Royal Astronomical Society.

Each spectrum went through the CIE 1931 color-matching functions, a standard model of how the eye responds to light. A Python package, ColorPy, turned the numbers into hex codes for a computer screen. As a test, a model of the Sun came out #FFF1EC, a white with a slight warm tint.

Where the models were not good enough, the team used spectra from the Sloan Digital Sky Survey (SDSS) and the Montreal White Dwarf Database (MWDD). A footnote asks readers to set monitors to sRGB mode and turn off blue-light filters.

Hollands said in the University of Warwick release that most stars behave like “perfect radiators.” “But nobody had really looked into what else might be possible for white dwarfs,” he said.

The atmospheres of the coolest white dwarfs can reach about 1 gram per cubic centimeter, roughly the density of water. There, a trace of carbon, calcium or sodium can block light over a very wide band.

Sodium on WD J1330+6435

Hollands and colleagues described WD J1330+6435 in Nature Astronomy in 2021. A team led by B. C. Kaiser reported it the same year in Science.

The lithium, sodium and calcium in its atmosphere came from rocky material in its old planetary system. Some planets survive the death of their star, and the giant planet WD 1856 b still orbits the white dwarf it outlived.

Back in 2021 Hollands and his co-authors did not have proper sodium line data and had to use a simpler approximation.

Koester’s code now has them, with sodium-helium calculations credited to V. Alekseev. The new fit puts the star at 4,240 Kelvin, about 7,170°F (3,970°C).

Sodium takes out the orange light and calcium takes out the violet, so the leftover light peaks near 500 nanometers. “The traces of sodium in the atmosphere of this white dwarf absorb the orange part of the spectrum, leaving behind a ghostly green glow,” Hollands said.

WD J0851+1543 and WD J1351+2645 also came out green, but pale. The authors wrote that they knew of no other star ever claimed to be green.

Salmon pink at 48 light-years

WD J1011+2845, also catalogued as LHS 2229, is 14.73 parsecs from the Sun, or about 48 light-years. Its temperature is estimated at 4,300 Kelvin, and it has a strong magnetic field.

Carbon from its core has been mixed up to the surface and forms C2 molecules, which block most light from 400 to 600 nanometers. Its observed spectrum gives the color code hex #FF8775, a deep salmon pink, and the paper calls it likely the reddest star known in perceived color.

The team looked at 36 carbon-rich white dwarfs and most were pink or purple. WD J0902+2010 is the pink star in an illustration Warwick released with the paper.

B. R. Oppenheimer and colleagues found WD J2356-2054 in 2001, and S. Blouin and colleagues first modeled it properly in 2019. Heavy sodium absorption makes it look vivid blue even though it is only about 4,000 Kelvin.

Of 15 cool white dwarfs with hydrogen and helium mixed together, several leaned cyan. WD J0039+3035 and WD J1403+4533 were the clearest cases.

Model spectra (top left), limb-darkening curves (bottom left) and color-accurate images (right) of three metal-polluted white dwarfs: the pale WD J0851+1543, the green WD J1330+6435 and the vivid blue WD J2356-2054. Credit: Hollands et al., RAS Techniques and Instruments (2026), CC BY 4.0. Click image to enlarge.

White dwarfs within 65 light-years

The team then went through all white dwarfs within 20 parsecs of the Sun, about 65 light-years. The paper counts 152 of them and the Warwick release gives 151. Sirius B, the closest white dwarf, is pale blue.

Five sat far from the normal range, with WD J0842-1347 and WD J2253-0646 showing yellow and WD J1011+2845, WD J1038-2040 and WD J2157-5100 showing pink. The paper puts that at 3.4 percent, and the release at about one in 30.

For the rest the team used color temperature, the scale used to sell light bulbs as warm or cool white. The median was 6,768 Kelvin, hex #F9F5FF, close to the 6,504 Kelvin white of a standard screen.

The authors noted, with some amusement, that the familiar image of blue white dwarfs does not hold up. Hot ones are bright and turn up first in surveys, so they are overcounted.

Gänsicke said one in 30 still means “a lot of oddly coloured stars” across the universe, but “the average white dwarf really is white.”

White dwarfs are what Sun-like stars leave after shedding their outer layers, as the Sun will one day inside a shell like the Helix Nebula. In the Lion Nebula, the white dwarf at the center is breaking up the gas it threw off.

Discs drawn for outreach

The paper ends with images of white dwarf discs made from Koester’s models and meant for outreach. They include limb darkening, the dimming toward a star’s edge, which in these stars also changes the color.

In the image of WD J1330+6435, the disc center is #D0FFB4, and the green fades toward the rim until the outermost pixels turn orange. WD J2356-2054 stays blue to the edge.

Warwick’s captions note that real stars would be many millions of times brighter than a screen. Only the hue is right.

Color-accurate simulated images of eight white dwarfs, each shown with its spectrum and temperature. Sirius B is pale blue and WD J1147-7457 orange; carbon molecules make WD J0902+2010 and WD J1011+2845 pink, while rocky debris gives WD J1535+1247, WD J0744+4649, the green WD J1330+6435 and the blue WD J2356-2054 their hues. Real stars would be millions of times brighter than a screen. Credit: Mark Hollands/University of Warwick. Click image to enlarge.

One noisy spectrum behind the green

The authors wrote that the WD J1330+6435 spectrum is somewhat noisy and the color could differ slightly. The 2021 model, with the older sodium data, also gave a green, #EAFFA1.

The carbon-rich and metal-rich stars were chosen to show the range of colors. They were not a fair sample, and the coolest mixed and carbon atmospheres are still hard to model.

WD J1644+1234, another lithium star, also looked greenish in a model. The model did not match the published spectrum, so the team dropped it.

Patrick Dufour refereed the paper. In the acknowledgements Hollands thanked J. J. Hermes, R. P. Ashley and C. E. Pugh for encouraging him to publish what he called an unconventional analysis.

For now WD J1330+6435 is the most vividly green star on record, as it would look to someone close enough to see its disc.

The full study was published in the journal RAS Techniques and Instruments.

APOD: 2026 October 31 – Night on a Spooky Planet

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

APOD

Astronomy Picture of the Day

Discover the cosmos! Each day a different image or photograph of our fascinating universe is featured, along with a brief explanation written by a professional astronomer.

Night on a Spooky Planet

Explanation: What spooky planet is this? Planet Earth on a dark and stormy night, of course. The ghostly scene was photographed at Hverir, a geothermally active area found along northeastern Iceland’s volcanic landscape. Triggered by solar activity, geomagnetic storms have unleashed energetic particles trapped in Earth’s magnetic field. Penetrating the planet’s upper atmosphere at high latitudes, the particles create auroral displays in the starry night sky. Ghostly towers of steam and gas are venting from local fumaroles to dance against the eerie, greenish northern lights. For now, auroral activity on planet Earth remains in a high state as the Sun is only in early phases of decline from the peak of its solar activity cycle. And soon, other ghostly apparitions may dance in your neighborhood. Happy Halloween!

Cisco Gives Engineer Unlimited AI Tokens To Rewrite Legacy Code

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
An anonymous reader quotes a report from Fortune: Gen Z can't catch a break. Entry-level jobs are vanishing; some 40% of bosses say they plan to hire fewer grads because AI can do the job cheaper, and young workers are routinely slammed for showing up late and refusing to put in overtime for free. Cisco's president and chief product officer Jeetu Patel isn't buying it -- he's just handed one of the company's biggest jobs to a 27-year-old he found hidden in the org chart "five levels" below him. "I've given him unlimited tokens because he's going to refactor code," Patel told Fortune at a small roundtable dinner. "So I'm like, you can go, you can go nuts." The "him" is Padmanabhan Krishnamurthy, or "Paddy," as Patel affectionately calls him. He joined Cisco in 2025 after the $429 billion tech giant acquired EzDubs, the real-time translation startup Krishnamurthy cofounded in 2023. And despite coming in as a former founder with CEO experience, he was, in Patel's words, "buried away in the organization doing something unstrategic." That changed when Patel dropped into Cisco's San Francisco office, and the pair got talking. Krishnamurthy reckoned he could rewrite Cisco's legacy code with a two-person team. Patel says even the most AI-forward people on his own team weren't thinking that way. So he has given himself four weeks to prove it, with the final goal of rewriting 98% of Cisco's legacy code within 24 months. Now, he's on the hunt for more Paddys: young hires who will "challenge the system and the status quo," or, as he puts it, be "an energizer bunny that just goes out and kind of revs everyone up." "The only way for us to keep accelerating is to make sure we're surrounded by people with a broad range of experience," said Patel. "Roughly one-third of my team is experienced, scaled operators who understand how the Cisco engine works. The second third are systems thinkers from the market who bring the outside-in perspective. And the final third is made of entrepreneurial founders, CEOs, and engineers who are AI-native who operate in founder mode." "Either of these groups by themselves aren't nearly as potent as when all three work together. It's what creates magic."

Read more of this story at Slashdot.

Kelly Clarkson Wins One & Loses One at 2026 Daytime Emmys

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Kelly Clarkson Show won outstanding daytime talk series at the 2026 Daytime Emmys on Friday (Oct. 30), though its star, Kelly Clarkson, lost outstanding daytime talk series host to Sherri Shepherd for Sherri.

Clarkson won in one or both of those categories in six of the show’s seven seasons on the air. She has won a total of nine Daytime Emmys in seven years – five in the talk series category and four for talk series host – compared to three Grammys in a recording career which has lasted more than 20 years. Clarkson wasn’t present at the ceremony, which was held at the Hollywood Palladium for the first time.

Related

Kelly Clarkson to End Daytime Talk Show After 7 Years

Kelly Clarkson Is a Double Winner Again at 2023 Daytime Emmys

Kelly Clarkson Wins One, Loses One at 2024 Daytime Emmys

Clarkson recently made headlines for an incident at a curiosity shop in Brooklyn where she maintained that she was treated poorly. But “Birdgate” had no bearing on the outcome here. There is just one round of Daytime Emmy voting, which determines both the nominations and the eventual winner. That round closed on June 10, four months before the incident.

The Kelly Clarkson Show won three other awards this year – best directing for a daytime studio non-fiction series; outstanding sound mixing and sound editing for a live daytime program; and outstanding art direction/set decoration/scenic design for a daytime program.

Ray Chew won outstanding music direction and composition for a daytime program for Macy’s Thanksgiving Day Parade. His award was accepted by his wife, who explained that he was busy working on Dancing With the Stars. Chew has also served in music direction capacities on American Idol, Saturday Night Live, the Emmy Awards, the BET Awards and the Grammy Awards’ Premiere Ceremony.

Keke Palmer won her first Daytime Emmy – outstanding daytime short form program as producer of Glam Through the Ages. Palmer has won two Primetime Emmys, for Keke Palmer’s Turnt Up With the Taylors and the reboot of Password.

Tracee Ellis Ross won outstanding travel and adventure program for Solo Traveling With Tracee Ellis Ross. It’s her first Emmy Award (Daytime or Primetime) following six Primetime Emmy nods for ABC’s blackish. Ross’ legendary mom, Diana Ross, has yet to win an Emmy.

Off Script With The Hollywood Reporter won outstanding arts and popular culture program, beating a pair of programs produced by their sister brand, Variety – Variety & CNN Actors on Actors and Variety Studio: Actors on Actors.

Star Jones won her first Daytime Emmy in a 35-year TV career – outstanding daytime personality – daily for Divorce Court, which she won alongside Corey Jovan. Jones thanked two late legends who she described as “her two mentors – Johnnie Cochran, who taught me about justice, and Barbara Walters, who taught me to ask the tough question.”

General Hospital won outstanding daytime drama series for the sixth consecutive year – and 19th time overall, both records. Runner-up for most total wins in the category is The Young and The Restless, with 11 wins.

Genie Francis received a lifetime achievement award to celebrate her soon-to-be 50th anniversary playing Laura Spencer on General Hospital. Her co-star on that show, Anthony Geary, the other half of the Luke & Laura storyline which took daytime drama to new heights in the early 1980s, died in December 2025. He was remembered in the In Memoriam segment alongside critic Gene Shalit and such other actors as June Lockhart and Sally Kirkland.

This is the second year in a row that the Daytime Emmys didn’t air on linear TV. Instead, the ceremony streamed live on the NATAS YouTube channel at YouTube.com/@theemmys and watch.TheEmmys.tv.

The full list of winners is available on the Daytime Emmys’ website. Here are the nominees in the two talk show categories and other categories of special interest to the music community, with winners marked.

Microduck, the Robot Pet That Imitates Life: Can We Really Grow Attached to a Machine?

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Imagine a small robotic duck walking around your home, reacting to its surroundings, and even picking up small objects with its beak. When it simulates sadness, it lowers its head and makes soft, melancholic sounds. When it expresses happiness, it produces higher-pitched, lively sounds accompanied by body movements. Its name is Microduck.

Standing just 25 centimeters tall and weighing less than 800 grams, it illustrates how advances in artificial intelligence and robotics are making increasingly sophisticated interactions between humans and machines possible.

One of its most curious features is its voice. Every Microduck has a unique sonic identity generated from its processor's serial number. As a result, no two units produce exactly the same sounds. It may seem like a minor detail, but it makes each robot recognizable, almost as if it had its own personality. Through different sounds and postures, Microduck can also simulate emotional states such as sadness and happiness.

Of course, the robot does not actually experience these emotions: it reproduces their outward expressions. Yet, the effect can be surprisingly convincing to anyone watching. There is also another interesting aspect: social behavior. When two or more Microducks come together, they can interact and, when appropriately configured, even perform a kind of chorus. Their behavior resembles that of animals that live in groups and communicate with one another.

But behind this playful appearance lies an enormous technological challenge. Microduck's software is open source, allowing developers to study how it works, modify it, and create new behaviors. Moreover, thanks to a digital twin and reinforcement learning, the robot can be trained in a virtual environment.

There, it can experiment with thousands of movements, fall, correct its mistakes, and try again. Cloud computing makes it possible to run large numbers of experiments in parallel, something that would require considerable time, money, and resources in the physical world. Once learned, these behaviors can be transferred to the real robot, an approach commonly known as sim-to-real transfer. This is how it can acquire unusual skills, such as learning to move on skates.

Another major challenge is efficiency. Microduck is equipped with fifteen motors, sensors, and a compact computing system. A neural network helps control its movements, continuously adjusting its balance and position. All of this must run on a small battery, which provides limited autonomy. Nature has been dealing with similar challenges for millions of years: animals perceive their environment, react, and move using energy obtained from food.

We tend to take these abilities for granted, yet reproducing them in machines remains extraordinarily difficult. Consider the complexity of developing increasingly autonomous robots capable of understanding instructions, reasoning about what to do, and translating decisions into precise physical movements. Combining artificial intelligence, perception, and physical action in small, affordable devices with limited energy consumption is one of the major technological challenges of the coming years.

This brings us to an interesting question: why did Microduck's developers choose these particular characteristics? Its distinctive voice, simulated emotions, interactions with other robots, and amusing movements seem directly inspired by the animal world. The goal is probably not just to make the product appealing, but also to encourage an emotional connection with its users.

A small robot like Microduck could anticipate a new generation of artificial pets, somewhere between intelligent toys and future household assistants.

It could keep us company, entertain us, and learn new behaviors through our interactions. It would also offer several practical advantages: it does not make a mess, does not need to be taken outside, does not get sick, and never requires a visit to the veterinarian. If we leave home for a week, we can simply turn it off and find it exactly where we left it when we return.

But a real pet is something profoundly different. A dog or a cat does not simply simulate emotions through a computer program. Animals are living beings, with instincts and biological needs, capable of experiencing pleasure, pain, and fear. They explore, seek companionship, and spontaneously react to what happens around them. They build relationships through experience, learning, and living together. The bond between humans and animals also develops through everyday care, shared habits, and trust built over time.

Above all, having a pet means accepting responsibility. An animal can become sick, grow old, and suffer. It needs us even when we are tired, busy, or would rather be doing something else. A robot, on the other hand, may offer many of the enjoyable aspects of companionship without requiring the same sacrifices. And perhaps that is precisely one of the reasons why devices like Microduck could become successful.

Yet there is a fundamental difference: simulating life is not the same as living it. An animal is not merely a collection of needs and responsibilities. It gives us affection, companionship, and moments of happiness. It gets to know us, recognizes our habits, and develops a relationship that changes over time.

Caring for it, facing difficulties together, and even knowing that one day we will have to say goodbye are all part of that relationship. We cannot simply turn off an animal when it becomes inconvenient, because it is not an object at our disposal. It is a living being with whom we share part of our existence.

But our relationship with an animal is only one part of its life. Every living organism belongs to a much larger cycle, in which different forms of life are interconnected and contribute to the balance of nature. Millions of years of evolution have shaped organisms capable of performing extraordinarily complex functions, often using surprisingly small amounts of energy and resources.

A robot can imitate an animal's voice, movements, and even its emotional expressions, but it does not participate in this biological cycle as a living organism. However sophisticated it becomes, it remains a collection of circuits, motors, and silicon chips.

Microduck is nevertheless an interesting technological achievement, showing how rapidly robotics is evolving, partly thanks to advances in artificial intelligence. I am convinced that similar devices will become successful and occupy an increasingly important place in our homes.

Yet, I must admit that this prospect also raises some concerns, particularly about the relationships we may develop with them. Perhaps the most interesting question is not how closely robots will come to resemble animals, but how willing we will be to grow attached to something that appears alive without actually being alive.
